is the set of coarse exponents which can be written as
\sigma\alpha+\tau\beta
, where
\sigma,\tau>0
(Definition
5.3
). Notice that while
\alpha
and
\beta
are only coarse exponents, the set
\Sigma
is still well-defined since we consider all positive linear combinations. Given a group
\mathcal{G}
and elements
g,h\in\mathcal{G}
, we use the following convention for group commutators:

[u,v]=v^{-1}u^{-1}vu.

Lemma 11.10
.

Fix a HAPHA. Let
\alpha
and
\beta
be non-proportional coarse Lyapunov exponents,
u\in N^{\alpha}
and
v\in N^{\beta}
. Then for every
x
, there exists a unique collection of elements
w_{i}:=\rho^{\alpha\beta}_{\gamma_{i}}(u,v,x)\in N^{\gamma_{i}}
, where
\gamma_{i}
ranges over all coarse Lyapunov exponents in
\Sigma(\alpha,\beta)
listed so that
(\alpha,\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\dots,\gamma_{n},\beta)
is the canonical circular ordering, satisfying:

w_{n}*\dots*w_{2}*w_{1}*[u,v]\cdot x=x.

Furthermore, the functions
\rho^{\alpha\beta}_{\gamma_{i}}
are continuous in all three variables, and satisfy the following equivariance property for any
g\in\mathbb{R}^{k}\times K
:

(11.1)

g_{*}\rho^{\alpha\beta}_{\gamma_{i}}(u,v,x)=\rho^{\alpha\beta}_{\gamma_{i}}(g_{*}u,g_{*}v,gx).

Proof.

Consider the points
y=[u,v]\cdot x
, and notice that if
a\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
satisfies
\alpha(a),\beta(a)<0
, then
d(a^{n}\cdot x,a^{n}\cdot y)\to 0
. In particular, if
\left\{a_{1},\dots,a_{m}\right\}
are elements such that
\Delta^{-}(\left\{a_{1},\dots,a_{m}\right\})=\left\{\alpha,\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n},\beta\right\}
(such a choice of
\left\{a_{i}\right\}
exists by Lemma
11.4
), then
y\in W^{s}_{(a_{1},\dots,a_{m})}(x)
. Hence, by
(HA-5)
there exist unique elements
u^{\prime}\in N^{\alpha}
,
v^{\prime}\in N^{\beta}
,
w_{i}\in N^{\gamma_{i}}
such that

(11.2)

y=u^{\prime}*w_{1}*\dots*w_{n}*v^{\prime}{\,{\cdot}\,}x.

Choose
a\in\ker\alpha
such that
\beta(a)<0
. Then since
\gamma_{i}\in\Sigma(\alpha,\beta)
for every
i
,
\gamma_{i}(a)<0
. In particular, using the automorphisms induced by
(HA-3)
and (
6.3
),

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}(a^{n})_{*}(u^{\prime}*w_{1}*\dots*w_{n}*v^{\prime})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}(a^{n})_{*}u^{\prime}*(a^{n})_{*}w_{1}\dots*(a^{n})_{*}v^{\prime}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle u^{\prime}*e*\dots*e*e

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle u^{\prime},

since for
v^{\prime}
and
w_{i}^{\prime}
, the automorphism
a_{*}
has eigenvalues determined by
e^{\beta(a)},e^{\gamma_{i}(a)}<1
, and the automorphism
a_{*}
on
N^{\alpha}
is the identity, since it is a graded homothety, and all eigenvalues are
e^{\alpha(a)}=1
. Therefore,
\lim_{n\to\infty}d(a^{n}x,a^{n}y)>0
unless
u^{\prime}=e
. However, since
(a^{n})_{*}[u,v]=[u,(a^{n})_{*}v]\to[u,e]=e
,
d(a^{n}x,a^{n}y)\to 0
. Therefore,
u^{\prime}=e
. Similarly,
v^{\prime}=e
by choosing
a\in\ker\beta
such that
\alpha(a)<0
. Since
u^{\prime},v^{\prime}=e
, (
11.2
) is the desired expression after multiplying by inverses.
∎

We have the following useful special case:

Corollary 11.11
.

If
\alpha
and
\beta
are non-proportional coarse Lyapunov exponents of a HAPHA and
\Sigma(\alpha,\beta)=\emptyset
, then
N^{\alpha}
and
N^{\beta}
commute.

Definition 11.12
.

The functions
\rho^{\alpha\beta}:N^{\alpha}\times N^{\beta}\to\displaystyle\prod_{i=1}^{\#\Sigma(\alpha,\beta)}N^{\gamma_{i}}
and
\rho^{\alpha,\beta}_{\gamma_{i}}:N^{\alpha}\times N^{\beta}\to N^{\gamma_{i}}
are called
geometric commutators
(we do not endow the target space with an algebraic structure, it is only a topological space). The element:

(11.3)

\rho^{\alpha,\beta}(u,v,x)*[u,v]

of
\mathcal{P}
is called a
commutator relation
at
x
. It is an element of the cycle subgroup at
x
.

Lemma 11.13
.

Let
\left\{\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n}\right\}
be a stable collection of coarse Lyapunov exponents listed in a circular ordering and
u_{i},v_{i}\in N^{\gamma_{i}}
. If
u_{1}*\dots*u_{n}\cdot x=v_{1}*\dots*v_{n}\cdot x
for every
x\in X
, then
v_{i}=u_{i}
for
i=1,\dots,n
.

Proof.

Suppose that
u_{1}*\dots*u_{r}\cdot x=v_{1}*\dots*v_{r}\cdot x
for every
x\in X
, where
u_{i},v_{i}\in N^{\alpha_{i}}
. Then

u_{1}*\dots*u_{r}*{v_{r}}^{-1}*\dots*{v_{1}}^{-1}

stabilizes every point of
X
. Picking some
a\in\ker\alpha_{r}
such that
\alpha_{i}(a)<0
for all
i=1,\dots,r-1
implies that

(a_{*}u_{1})*\dots*(a_{*}u_{r-1})*u_{r}*{v_{r}}^{-1}*(a_{*}v_{r-1})^{-1}\dots*(a_{*}v_{1})^{-1}

also stabilizes every point of
X
. Letting
\alpha_{i}(a)\to\infty
,
i<r
implies that
u_{r}{v_{r}}^{-1}
stabilizes every point of
X
. Since the action of
N^{\alpha_{1}}
is faithful,
u_{r}=v_{r}
. Iterating this procedure by choosing
a_{i}\in\ker\beta_{i}
such that
\alpha_{j}(a_{i})<0
for
j=1,\dots,i-1
inductively shows
u_{i}=v_{i}
,
i=1,\dots,r-1
.
∎

11.4.
Groups generated by opposite coarse weights

In this section, we study the interaction of the groups
N^{\alpha}
and
N^{-\alpha}
, where
\alpha\in\Delta
is a coarse weight such that
-\alpha\in\Delta
. We will show that they fit into a Lie group action, and establish certain structural features.

Lemma 11.14
.

If
y\in\overline{\ker\alpha\cdot x}
, then
\overline{\ker\alpha\cdot y}\subset\overline{\ker\alpha\cdot x}
.

Proof.

Suppose that
z\in\overline{\ker\alpha\cdot y}
and
a\in\ker\alpha
be such that
d(ay,z)<\varepsilon
. Since
a:X\to X
is continuous, we may choose
\delta>0
such that if
d(y,y^{\prime})<\delta
, then
d(ay^{\prime},z)<\varepsilon
. Then choose
b\in\ker\alpha
such that
d(bx,y)<\delta
. Then by construction,
d(abx,z)<\varepsilon
and
z\in\overline{\ker\alpha\cdot x}
.
∎

The following lemma gives a topological analogue of an abstract ergodic decomposition. We will apply it in different settings for the action of
\ker\alpha
.

Lemma 11.15
.

Fix
\alpha\in\Delta
. There is an
\mathbb{R}^{k}
-invariant residual set of points
x_{0}\in X
such that
(K\times\ker\alpha)\cdot x_{0}
is dense and either

(1)

\ker\alpha\cdot x_{0}
is dense, or

(2)

\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}(m):=m\cdot\overline{\ker\alpha\cdot x_{0}}
,
m\in K
are a family of closed sets that partition
X
and each atom is saturated by
W^{\beta}
-leaves for every
\beta\in\Delta
(including
\beta=\alpha
). The indexing of the partition
\left\{\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}(m):m\in K\right\}
by
m
depends on
x_{0}
, but the partition itself is independent of the choice of
x_{0}
from the residual set.

In case (2), for every
a\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
and
m\in K
, there exists
m^{\prime}\in K
such that
a\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}(m)=\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}(m^{\prime})
.

Proof.

We wish to apply Lemma
F.6
. We begin by considering the partition
\mathcal{W}
induced by the equivalence relation that
x\sim y
if and only if there exists
a\in\ker\alpha
such that
ax
and
y
are connected by a path in the foliations
W^{\beta}
,
\beta\not=\alpha
with finitely many legs. Our application of Lemma
F.6
will rely on whether we are assuming
(HA-7)
(a) or
(HA-7)
(b).

Under assumption
(HA-7)
(a),
K\mathcal{W}(x)=X
for all
x\in X
. We may then apply Lemma
F.6
with
\mathcal{G}=\left\{e\right\}
and get that a partition consisting of sets of the form
\overline{\mathcal{W}(mx_{0})}
. We may without loss of generality assume that
x_{0}
is a point which satisfies that
\mathcal{W}(x_{0})=\overline{(\ker\alpha)\cdot x_{0}}
, since this condition is generic when the action is totally recurrent (see
(HA-4)
,
[
72
, Lemma 10.2]
and Lemma
F.8
). The results follows.

Under assumption
(HA-7)
(b), we use the same equivalence relation to define the partition
\mathcal{W}
, but introduce the group
\mathcal{G}
. Let
L\subset\mathbb{R}^{k}
be any line transverse to
\ker\alpha
, and let
\mathcal{G}=L\ltimes(N^{\alpha}*N^{-\alpha})
be the semidirect product of
L
and the group freely generated by
N^{\alpha}
and
N^{-\alpha}
. Note that since geometric commutators of
\alpha
-legs with
\beta
-legs,
\beta\not=\alpha
only produce new legs
\gamma
with
\gamma\not=\alpha
, it follows that for any finite path
\rho
in the foliations
W^{\beta}
,
\beta\not=\alpha
which begins at a point
x
and ends at a point
y
,
hy
is the endpoint of some path
\rho^{\prime}
which begins at
hx
for all
h\in\mathcal{G}
. That is,
h\mathcal{W}(x)=\mathcal{W}(hx)
for all
h\in K\ltimes\mathcal{G}
. Finally, by assumption
K\mathcal{W}(x_{0})\supset(K\times\ker\alpha)\cdot x_{0}
is dense for some
x_{0}
. To apply Lemma
F.6
it remains to show property (*). This follows by taking the compact subsets of
\mathcal{G}
which consist of a short
L
-leg, a single short
\alpha
-leg and a single short
-\alpha
-leg, listed in that order. Then
(HA-7)
(b), the local product structure assumption, yields (*). The remainder of the proof is as in the previous case.
∎

Fix a coarse weight
\alpha\in\Delta
such that
-\alpha\in\Delta
as well. Our next immediate goal is to fit the actions of
N^{\alpha}
and
N^{-\alpha}
into the action of a single Lie group
G_{\alpha}
.

Lemma 11.16
.

Let
\left\{\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}(m):m\in K\right\}
denote the partition of
X
into
\ker\alpha
-orbit closures from Lemma
11.15
. There exists a continuous fiber bundle
\mathcal{G}_{\alpha}
over
K
such that

(1)

The fiber above
m\in K
is a simply connected Lie group
G_{\alpha}(m)
.

(2)

For every
m\in K
,
N^{\alpha}
and
N^{-\alpha}
embed into
G_{\alpha}(m)
, and generate
G_{\alpha}(m)
.

(3)

For each
m\in K
,
G_{\alpha}(m)
acts on
\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}(m)\subset X
.

(4)

The restrictions of the
G_{\alpha}(m)
-action to the embeddings of
N^{\alpha}
and
N^{-\alpha}
coincide with the actions from
(HA-2)
.

(5)

The vector bundle with fibers
\operatorname{Lie}(G_{\alpha}(m))
is a continuous vector bundle over
K
, with
\operatorname{Lie}(N^{\alpha})
and
\operatorname{Lie}(N^{-\alpha})
as continuous subbundles.

Proof.

Fix
x\in X
, and consider the stabilizer of a point,
\mathcal{C}_{\alpha}(x)
, under the action of the free product
N^{\alpha}*N^{-\alpha}
on
X
. Then
\ker\alpha
takes any cycle with legs only from
N^{\alpha}
and
N^{-\alpha}
to the same cycle at a new basepoint (since by
(HA-3)
,
\ker\alpha
acts trivially on every leg), i.e.
\mathcal{C}_{\alpha}(x)=a_{*}\mathcal{C}_{\alpha}(x)=\mathcal{C}_{\alpha}(a\cdot x)
. Choose
x_{0}
as in Lemma
11.15
such that
x_{0}
has a dense
\mathbb{R}^{k}
-orbit.
Since the atoms of
\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}
are saturated by coarse Lyapunov leaves,
N^{\alpha}*N^{-\alpha}
-orbits are contained in a single
\ker\alpha
-orbit closure. Hence, the cycles at any point
x\in\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}(m)
contain those of
m\cdot x_{0}
. Therefore, by Corollary
6.8
at each
m\in K
the group
\bar{G}_{\alpha}(m)=(N^{\alpha}*N^{-\alpha})/\mathcal{C}_{\alpha}(m\cdot x_{0})
is Lie, and has a canonical continuous action
\bar{G}_{\alpha}(m)\curvearrowright\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}(m)
into which the group actions of
N^{\alpha}
and
N^{-\alpha}
canonically embed. Let
G_{\alpha}(m)
denote the universal cover of
\bar{G}_{\alpha}(m)
, so that
G_{\alpha}(m)
also acts on
\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}(m)
, and is determined by its Lie algebra. In particular, we get two key features: that
\operatorname{Lie}(N^{\alpha})
and
\operatorname{Lie}(N^{-\alpha})
can both be considered subalgebras of
G_{\alpha}(m)
, and
G_{\alpha}(m)
acts locally freely (by definition, the evaluation map
g\mapsto gmx_{0}
is a continuous bijection for
\bar{G}_{\alpha}(m)
).

Finally, we wish to show that
\mathcal{G}_{\alpha}
has the structure of a continuous fiber bundle. Indeed, it is a trivial fiber bundle. If
\rho\in N^{\alpha}*N^{-\alpha}
and
m\in K
, and
m_{*}:N^{\alpha}*N^{-\alpha}\to N^{\alpha}*N^{-\alpha}
is the automorphism from
(HA-3)
, the trivialization given by

\phi:G_{\alpha}(e)\times K\to\mathcal{G}_{\alpha}\qquad\phi(\rho\,\mathcal{C}_{\alpha}(e),m)=(m_{*}\rho)\mathcal{C}_{\alpha}(m)

Since
\mathcal{G}_{\alpha}
is a continuous fiber bundle, it follows immediately that the Lie algebras of the fibers also vary continuously.
∎

Lemma 11.17
.

There exists a simply connected Lie group
G_{\alpha}
and a continuous group action
G_{\alpha}\curvearrowright X
such that

(1)

\operatorname{Lie}(G_{\alpha})\cong\operatorname{Lie}(N^{\alpha})\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{0}\oplus\operatorname{Lie}(N^{-\alpha})
for some subalgebra
\mathfrak{g}_{0}
,

(2)

the inclusion of
\operatorname{Lie}(N^{\pm\alpha})
in
\operatorname{Lie}(G_{\alpha})
induces a local isomorphism from
N^{\pm\alpha}
onto its image,

(3)

the action of
\exp_{G_{\alpha}}(\operatorname{Lie}(N^{\pm\alpha}))
coincides with the existing action of
N^{\pm\alpha}
,

(4)

the subgroups
\exp_{G_{\alpha}}(\operatorname{Lie}(N^{\alpha}))
and
\exp_{G_{\alpha}}(\operatorname{Lie}(N^{-\alpha}))
generate
G_{\alpha}
, and

(5)

the action of
G_{0,\alpha}:=\exp_{G_{\alpha}}(\mathfrak{g}_{0})
commutes with the
\mathbb{R}^{k}
-action.

Proof.

Consider the groups
G_{\alpha}(m)=(N^{\alpha}*N^{-\alpha})/\mathcal{C}_{\alpha}(m)
from Lemma
11.16
. We will show the existence of a normal subgroup
\mathcal{C}_{0}\subset N^{\alpha}*N^{-\alpha}
such that
(N^{\alpha}*N^{-\alpha})/\mathcal{C}_{0}
is a Lie group and
\mathcal{C}_{0}\subset\mathcal{C}_{\alpha}(m)
for every
m\in K
. This will imply that there is a global group action
G_{\alpha}\curvearrowright X
(since in this case,
G_{\alpha}(m)
factors through a common quotient of the free product
N^{\alpha}*N^{-\alpha}
). Recall that if
g\in\mathbb{R}^{k}\times K
,
g
normalizes the
N^{\pm\alpha}
-actions. Let
g_{*}
denote the induced automorphism of
N^{\alpha}*N^{-\alpha}
. Any globally-defined map preserving the coarse Lyapunov foliations will take cycles to cycles, so
\mathcal{C}_{\alpha}(g\cdot n)=g_{*}\mathcal{C}_{\alpha}(n)
. Since
g_{*}
is an automorphism of
N^{\alpha}*N^{-\alpha}
taking
\mathcal{C}_{\alpha}(m)
to
\mathcal{C}_{\alpha}(g\cdot m)
,
g_{*}
induces an isomorphism
g_{*}:G_{\alpha}(m)\to G_{\alpha}(g\cdot m)
.

As a remark, note that this determines the isomorphism class of
G_{\alpha}(m)
, but that this is insufficient for our purposes. To obtain a group action on
X
, we need to know the cycles
\mathcal{C}_{\alpha}(m)
are constant, which is to say that the generating relations for the groups are the same.

We now consider the Lie algebra structure of
G_{\alpha}(m)
. Notice that
N^{\pm\alpha}
has a grading that corresponds to the Oseledets splitting,
\operatorname{Lie}(N^{\alpha})=E^{\alpha}\oplus E^{c_{2}\alpha}\oplus\dots\oplus E^{c_{\ell_{1}}\alpha}
and
\operatorname{Lie}(N^{-\alpha})=E^{-d_{1}\alpha}\oplus E^{-d_{2}\alpha}\oplus{\cdots\oplus}E^{-d_{\ell_{2}}\alpha}
.
Suppose that
{Z}\in E^{c_{i}\alpha}
and
Y\in E^{-d_{j}\alpha}
. Then for any
x\in\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}(m)
,
\exp_{G_{\alpha}(m)}(t[{Z},Y]_{G_{\alpha}(m)})x
is a continuous curve in
X
, but a priori only a continuous curve. For each
a\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
,
a_{*}
is an isomorphism between
G_{\alpha}(m)
and
G_{\alpha}(m^{\prime})
, where
m^{\prime}
is such that
a\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}(m)=\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}(m^{\prime})
. Let us consider the asymptotic behavior
\exp_{G_{\alpha}(m)}(t[{Z},Y]_{G_{\alpha}(m)})x
. If
d_{j}>c_{i}
, then as
\alpha(a)\to\infty
:

\displaystyle d(ax,a\exp_{G_{\alpha}(m)}(t[{Z},Y]_{G_{\alpha}(m)})x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle d(\exp_{G_{\alpha}(m^{\prime})}(ta_{*}[{Z},Y]_{G_{\alpha}(m)})ax,ax)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle d(\exp_{G_{\alpha}(m^{\prime})}(te^{(c_{i}-d_{j})\alpha(a)}[{Z},Y]_{G_{\alpha}(m^{\prime})})ax,ax)

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle 0.

Importantly, note that we do not yet know the precise rate of convergence using the distance on
X
, since the group action itself is only continuous. However, if
d_{j}>c_{i}
, then the curve
\exp_{G_{\alpha}(m)}(t[{Z},Y]_{G_{\alpha}(m)})
is contained in the
N^{-\alpha}
-orbit by Lemma
11.9
(and similarly for the
N^{\alpha}
-orbit for
c_{i}>d_{j}
).

Since the
G_{\alpha}(m)
-action has a locally free orbit, we conclude that for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
,
\exp(t[Z,Y]_{G_{\alpha}(m)})\in N^{\pm\alpha}
whenever
c_{i}\not=d_{j}
. That is,
[{Z},Y]_{G_{\alpha}(m)}\in\operatorname{Lie}(N^{\pm\alpha})
.

Choose any continuous family of norms on
\operatorname{Lie}(G_{\alpha}(m))
such that for
g\in K
, the maps
g_{*}:\operatorname{Lie}(G_{\alpha}(m))\to\operatorname{Lie}(G_{\alpha}(gm))
induced by
(HA-3)
are isometric (one may average over the closed subgroup of
K
which fixes
\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}(m)
).

Assume that
c_{i}>d_{j}
, so
[{Z},Y]_{G_{\alpha}(m)}\in\operatorname{Lie}(N^{\alpha})
(the opposite case is identical). Choose any
b\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
such that
\alpha(b)>0
, and let
m\in K
. Then
b\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}(m)=\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}(gm)
for some
g\in K
, and
bg^{-1}
fixes
\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}(m)
. Then
(bg^{-1})_{*}
is a harnessed automorphism of
G_{\alpha}(m)
. Let
{Z}^{\prime}:=g_{*}{Z}
and
Y^{\prime}:=g_{*}Y
. Then, using that the
K
-action preserves the norms:

\displaystyle(bg^{-1})_{*}[{Z},Y]_{G_{\alpha}(m)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle[(bg^{-1})_{*}{Z},(bg^{-1})_{*}Y]_{G_{\alpha}(m)},\mbox{ so}

\displaystyle\left|\left|(bg^{-1})_{*}[{Z},Y]_{G_{\alpha}(m)}\right|\right|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left|\left|[e^{c_{i}\alpha(b)}{Z}^{\prime},e^{-d_{j}\alpha(b)}Y^{\prime}]_{G_{\alpha}(m)}\right|\right|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{(c_{i}-d_{j})\alpha(b)}\left|\left|[{Z}^{\prime},Y^{\prime}]_{G_{\alpha}({gm})}\right|\right|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{(c_{i}-d_{j})\alpha(b)}\left|\left|[{Z},Y]_{G_{\alpha}(m)}\right|\right|.

Moreover since
(bg^{-1})_{\ast}
preserves the Oseledets splitting in
\operatorname{Lie}(N^{\pm\alpha})
, by a similar proof we have

\displaystyle\left|\left|(bg^{-1})^{n}_{*}[{Z},Y]_{G_{\alpha}(m)}\right|\right|=e^{n(c_{i}-d_{j})\alpha(b)}\left|\left|[{Z},Y]_{G_{\alpha}(m)}\right|\right|.

Since
[{Z},Y]_{G_{\alpha}(m)}\in\operatorname{Lie}(N^{\alpha})
, this implies that
[{Z},Y]_{G_{\alpha}(m)}
is in the direct sum of generalized eigenspaces of
(bg^{-1})_{*}
with eigenvalues whose moduli are
e^{(c_{i}-d_{j})\alpha(b)}
and hence that
[{Z},Y]_{G_{\alpha}(m)}\in E^{(c_{i}-d_{j})\alpha}
.

In the case when
c_{i}=d_{j}
, the flow generated by
[{Z},Y]_{G_{\alpha}(m)}
commutes with the
\mathbb{R}^{k}
action, as claimed. Let
\mathfrak{g}_{0}(m)\subset\operatorname{Lie}(G_{\alpha}(m))
denote the vector subspace of the Lie algebra spanned by elements of the form
[Z,Y]_{G_{\alpha}(m)}
for some
Z\in E^{c_{i}\alpha}
and
Y\in E^{-c_{i}\alpha}
. Since
G_{\alpha}(m)
is generated by
N^{\alpha}
and
N^{-\alpha}
, we wish to establish the vector space decomposition

(11.4)

\operatorname{Lie}(G_{\alpha}(m))=\operatorname{Lie}(N^{\alpha})\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{0}(m)\oplus\operatorname{Lie}(N^{-\alpha}).

It suffices to check that

(i)

[\mathfrak{g}_{0}(m),E^{c_{i}\alpha}]\subset E^{c_{i}\alpha}
(similarly for
E^{-d_{j}\alpha}
), and

(ii)

[\mathfrak{g}_{0}(m),\mathfrak{g}_{0}(m)]\subset\mathfrak{g}_{0}(m)
(ie,
\mathfrak{g}_{0}(m)
is a subalgebra).

In fact, we show a stronger version of (i), that if
W=[Z,Y]\in\mathfrak{g}_{0}(m)
, and
Z^{\prime}\in E^{c_{j}\alpha}
, then
[W,Z^{\prime}]\in E^{c_{j}\alpha}
. This follows since
a_{*}[W,Z^{\prime}]=[a_{*}W,a_{*}Z^{\prime}]=e^{c_{j}\alpha(a)}[W,Z^{\prime}]
. Hence
[W,Z^{\prime}]
is in the
E^{c_{j}\alpha}
-eigenspace of
a_{*}
, and hence part of
\operatorname{Lie}(N^{\alpha})
by Lemma
11.9
(since dynamically it contracts for any
a
such that
\alpha(a)<0
). It follows that it must be in the corresponding eigenspace.

To see (ii), note that if
Z_{1}\in E^{c_{i}\alpha}
,
Y_{1}\in E^{-c_{i}\alpha}
,
Z_{2}\in E^{-c_{j}\alpha}
and
Y_{2}\in E^{-c_{j}\alpha}
, then by the Jacobi identity

(11.5)

[[Z_{1},Y_{1}],[Z_{2},Y_{2}]]=-[[[Z_{2},Y_{2}],Z_{1}],Y_{1}]-[[Y_{1},[Z_{2},Y_{2}]],Z_{1}].

By (i), this is a sum of elements of
\mathfrak{g}_{0}(m)
, so
\mathfrak{g}_{0}(m)
is a subalgebra.
Therefore,
\operatorname{Lie}(G_{\alpha}(m))
has a canonical splitting
\operatorname{Lie}(N^{\alpha})\oplus{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathfrak{g}_{0}(m)}\oplus\operatorname{Lie}(N^{-\alpha})
.

Finally, we wish to show the independence of
\mathfrak{g}_{0}(m)
on
m
. Given
m\in K
, let

\omega_{m}:\bigoplus_{\alpha}\bigoplus_{i}E^{c_{i}\alpha}\wedge E^{-c_{i}\alpha}\to\mathfrak{g}_{0}(m)\qquad\mbox{be defined by }\qquad\omega_{m}(Y\wedge Z)=[Y,Z]_{G_{\alpha}(m)}.

Notice that
\omega_{m}
is onto
\mathfrak{g}_{0}(m)
for every
m
. We claim that
\ker\omega_{m}
is a constant subspace of
\omega_{m}
. Indeed, let
a\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
,
\sum Y_{\ell}\wedge Z_{\ell}\in\ker\omega_{m}
,
m\in K
and
m^{\prime}
be such that
a\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}(m)=\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}(m^{\prime})
. Since
a_{*}:\mathfrak{g}_{0}(m)\to\mathfrak{g}_{0}(m^{\prime})
is a Lie algebra isomorphism, we get

0=a_{*}\omega_{m}\left(\sum Y_{\ell}\wedge Z_{\ell}\right)=\sum a_{*}[Y_{\ell},Z_{\ell}]_{G_{\alpha}(m)}=\sum[a_{*}Y_{\ell},a_{*}Z_{\ell}]_{G_{\alpha}(m^{\prime})}\\
=[e^{c_{i_{\ell}}\alpha(a)}Y_{\ell},e^{-c_{i_{\ell}}\alpha(a)}Z]_{G_{\alpha}(m^{\prime})}=\sum[Y_{\ell},Z_{\ell}]_{G_{\alpha}(m^{\prime})}=\omega_{m^{\prime}}\left(\sum Y_{\ell}\wedge Z_{\ell}\right).

Thus,
m\mapsto\ker\omega_{m}
is
\mathbb{R}^{k}
-invariant. Notice that the dimension is constant since the groups
G_{\alpha}(m)
are all isomorphic (and hence
\mathfrak{g}_{0}(m)
must have constant dimension). Since the Lie algebra structures vary continuously, it follows that
\omega_{m}
is a continuous map from the bundle with fibers
\operatorname{Lie}(G_{\alpha}(m))
over
K
. In particular, we get that
\ker\omega_{m}
is independent of
m
.

Define
\psi_{m,m^{\prime}}:\mathfrak{g}_{0}(m)\to\mathfrak{g}_{0}(m^{\prime})
by

\psi_{m,m^{\prime}}(\omega_{m}(Y\wedge Z)):=\omega_{m^{\prime}}(Y\wedge Z),

whenever
Z\in E^{c_{i}\alpha}
and
Y\in E^{-c_{i}\alpha}
whenever
E^{c_{i}\alpha}
and
E^{-c_{i}\alpha}
are both Oseledets spaces. Since the
\omega_{m}
are always onto and have a common kernel, this map is well-defined. Note that since the groups
G_{\alpha}(m)
are all isomorphic, the dimension of each
\mathfrak{g}_{0}(m)
is always the same, and hence
\psi_{m,m^{\prime}}
is always an isomorphism of vector spaces. Hence, the maps
\psi_{m,m^{\prime}}
give an identification of the Lie algebras
\mathfrak{g}_{0}(m)
for any
m\in K
.

Extend
\psi_{m,m^{\prime}}
to
\Psi_{m,m^{\prime}}:\operatorname{Lie}(G_{\alpha}(m))\to\operatorname{Lie}(G_{\alpha}(m^{\prime}))
using the decomposition (
11.4
), setting
\Psi_{m,m^{\prime}}|_{N^{\pm\alpha}}=\mathrm{Id}
. Then
\Psi_{m,m^{\prime}}
is a vector space isomorphism for any
m,m^{\prime}
, and we wish to show that it is a Lie algebra isomorphism. Notice that it suffices to show that

[Y,Z]_{G_{\alpha}(m)}=[Y,Z]_{G_{\alpha}(m^{\prime})},\;[W,Y]_{G_{\alpha}(m)}=[\psi_{m,m^{\prime}}W,Y]_{G_{\alpha}(m^{\prime})},\mbox{ and }[W,Z]_{G_{\alpha}(m)}=[\psi_{m,m^{\prime}}W,Z]_{G_{\alpha}(m^{\prime})}

for any
m,m^{\prime}\in K
,
{Z}\in E^{c_{i}\alpha}
,
Y\in E^{-d_{j}\alpha}
and
W\in\mathfrak{g}_{0}(m)
. Indeed, since
\operatorname{Lie}(N^{\pm\alpha})
are already subalgebras, the only missing brackets are of the form
\psi_{m,m^{\prime}}[W,W^{\prime}]_{G_{\alpha}(m)}=[\psi_{m,m^{\prime}}W,\psi_{m,m^{\prime}}W^{\prime}]_{G_{\alpha}(m^{\prime})}
. But these can be computed from (
11.5
), so it suffices to check the relations above.

We first prove the claim when
\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}(m^{\prime})=a\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}(m)
. We will leverage the fact that
[{Z},Y]_{G_{\alpha}(m)}\in E^{(c_{i}-d_{j})\alpha(a)}
if
c_{i}\not=d_{j}
.
Given
Z\in E^{c_{i}\alpha}
and
Y\in E^{-d_{j}\alpha}
, with
c_{i}\not=d_{j}
, we see that since
a_{*}
is a Lie algebra isomorphism,

\displaystyle[{Z},Y]_{G_{\alpha}(m)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(a^{-1})_{*}[a_{*}{Z},a_{*}Y]_{G_{\alpha}(m^{\prime})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{(d_{j}-c_{i})\alpha(a)}[e^{c_{i}\alpha(a)}{Z},e^{-d_{j}\alpha(a)}Y]_{G_{\alpha}(m^{\prime})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle[{Z},Y]_{G_{\alpha}(m^{\prime})}.

Now, if
c_{i}=d_{j}
, the
[Z,Y]_{G_{\alpha}(m)}\in\mathfrak{g}_{0}(m)
and commutes with the
\mathbb{R}^{k}
-action. Hence if
W=[Y^{\prime},Z^{\prime}]_{G_{\alpha}(m)}\in\mathfrak{g}_{0}(m)
is the image of a primitive element of
E^{c_{i}\alpha}\wedge E^{-c_{i}\alpha}
under
\omega_{m}
, we can write

\displaystyle[W,Y]_{G_{\alpha}(m)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle[[Y^{\prime},Z^{\prime}]_{G_{\alpha}(m)},Y]_{G_{\alpha}(m)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(a^{-1})_{*}[[a_{*}Y^{\prime},a_{*}Z^{\prime}]_{G_{\alpha}(m^{\prime})},a_{*}Y]_{G_{\alpha}(m^{\prime})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{-c_{i}\alpha(a)}[\psi_{m,m^{\prime}}W,e^{c_{i}\alpha(a)}Y]_{G_{\alpha}(m^{\prime})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle[\psi_{m,m^{\prime}}W,Y]_{G_{\alpha}(m^{\prime})}.

The last case is identical. Therefore, the map
\Psi_{m,m^{\prime}}
is an isomorphism whenever
\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}(m)
and
\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}(m^{\prime})
belong to the same
\mathbb{R}^{k}
-orbit. Since
G_{\alpha}(m)
is simply connected, these isomorphisms lift to isomorphisms from
G_{\alpha}(m)\to G_{\alpha}(m^{\prime})
. Since they are the identity on
N^{\pm\alpha}
,
\mathcal{C}^{c}_{\alpha}(x_{0})
, the space of (contractible) cycles in
N^{\alpha}
and
N^{-\alpha}
is constant on a dense set by
(HA-1)
. The sets
\mathcal{C}_{\alpha}(x)
are semi-continuously varying in the following sense: if
\lim_{n\to\infty}x_{n}=x
, and
\sigma_{n}\in\mathcal{C}_{\alpha}(x_{n})
is a sequence of cycles converging to a cycle
\sigma
, then
\sigma\in\mathcal{C}(x)
. By density of the
\mathbb{R}^{k}
orbit and this semicontinuity,
\mathcal{C}_{\alpha}(x_{0})
is contained in
\mathcal{C}_{\alpha}(x)
for every
x\in X
. Therefore, there is a Lie group
G_{\alpha}=(N^{\alpha}*N^{-\alpha})/\mathcal{C}_{\alpha}(x_{0})
which acts on the total space
X
, as described.
∎

12.
Polynomial forms of geometric commutators

Recall the geometric commutator functions
\rho^{\alpha,\beta}:N^{\alpha}\times N^{\beta}\times X\to\prod_{i=\#\Sigma(\alpha,\beta)}^{1}N^{\gamma_{i}}
from Lemma
11.10
and Definition
11.12
.

Theorem 12.1
.

If
\mathbb{R}^{k}\times K\curvearrowright X
is a HAPHA satisfying the assumptions of Theorem
11.8
, then the functions
\rho^{\alpha,\beta}(u,v,x)
are independent of
x
.

We will in fact show in Corollary
12.12
that the functions
\rho^{\alpha,\beta}(u,v,x)
are polynomials independent of
x
using natural coordinates on
N^{\alpha}
and
N^{\beta}
.

12.1.
Reduction to Oseledets subspaces

If
\alpha
is a coarse Lyapunov exponent, recall from the definition of a HAPHA that
\operatorname{Lie}(N^{\alpha})
splits as a direct sum of Oseledets subspaces. That is,
\operatorname{Lie}(N^{\alpha})=E^{c_{1}^{\alpha}\alpha}\oplus\dots\oplus E^{c_{\ell(\alpha)}^{\alpha}\alpha}
. Let
e(u)=\exp(u)
for ease of notation, and
\log
denote its inverse (which exists because
N^{\alpha}
is nilpotent). Then by
(HA-3)
, if
u\in E^{c_{i}^{\alpha}\alpha}
,
a\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
,

(12.1)

a_{*}e(u)=e(e^{c_{i}^{\alpha}\alpha(a)}u).

Definition 12.2
.

Given Oseledets spaces
E^{c_{i}^{\alpha}\alpha}
and
E^{c_{j}^{\beta}\beta}
, let
\hat{\rho}^{c_{i}^{\alpha}\alpha,c_{j}^{\beta}\beta}_{{c_{m}^{\gamma}}\gamma}:E^{c_{i}^{\alpha}\alpha}\times E^{c_{j}^{\beta}\beta}\times X\to\operatorname{Lie}(N^{\gamma})
be defined by

\hat{\rho}^{c_{i}^{\alpha}\alpha,c_{j}^{\beta}\beta}_{c_{m}^{\gamma}\gamma}(u,v,x)=\pi_{m}^{\gamma}(\log\rho^{\alpha,\beta}_{\gamma}(e(u),e(v),x)),

where
\pi_{m}^{\gamma}:\operatorname{Lie}(N^{\gamma})\to E^{c_{m}^{\gamma}\gamma}
is the projection onto the corresponding Oseledets space induced by the Oseledets splitting.

We now turn to the last technical assumption which appears in Theorem
11.8
. If
c_{m}^{\gamma}\gamma=\sigma\,c_{i}^{\alpha}\alpha+\tau\,c_{j}^{\beta}\beta
for some
\sigma,\tau>0
, we call
\sigma
and
\tau
the
Lyapunov coefficients
of
c_{m}^{\gamma}\gamma
with respect to
c_{i}^{\alpha}\alpha
and
c_{j}^{\beta}\beta
. We say that an action has
integral Lyapunov coefficients
if for any non proportional
\alpha,\beta\in\Delta
, any
c_{i}^{\alpha}\alpha\in[\alpha],c_{j}^{\beta}\beta\in[\beta]
,
\hat{\rho}^{c_{i}^{\alpha}\alpha,c_{j}^{\beta}\beta}_{c_{m}^{\gamma}\gamma}\equiv 0
whenever both Lyapunov coefficients (with respect to
c_{i}^{\alpha}\alpha
and
c_{j}^{\beta}\beta
) are less than 1.

Definition 12.3
.

We denote by

\Omega:=\{c^{\alpha}_{i}\alpha,\alpha\in\Delta\}

the set of all Lyapunov functionals. For
\alpha,\beta\in\Delta
, similar to
\Sigma(\alpha,\beta)
, let
\Omega(\alpha,\beta)\subset\Omega
be the set of
c^{\gamma}_{k}\gamma\in\Omega
such that
c^{\gamma}_{k}\gamma=\sigma c^{\alpha}_{i}\alpha+\tau c^{\beta}_{j}\beta
for some
\sigma,\tau>0
.

Lemma 12.4
.

If for every pair of Oseledets subspaces
E^{c_{i}^{\alpha}\alpha},E^{c_{j}^{\beta}\beta}
with
\alpha
and
\beta
non-proportional, the functions
\hat{\rho}^{c_{i}^{\alpha}\alpha,c_{j}^{\beta}\beta}_{c_{m}^{\gamma}\gamma}
are independent of
x
, then the functions
\rho^{\alpha,\beta}
are independent of
x
for every
\alpha,\beta\in\Delta
.

Proof.

Let
u\in N^{\alpha}
and
v\in N^{\beta}
. Then we may write

u=e(u_{1})e(u_{2})\cdots e(u_{\ell(\alpha)})\mbox{ and }v=e(v_{1})e(v_{2})\cdots e(v_{\ell(\beta)}),

where
u_{i}\in E^{c_{i}^{\alpha}\alpha}
and
v_{j}\in E^{c_{j}^{\beta}\beta}
. We wish to compute
[u,v]
using commutator relations coming from commutators from the exponentials of Oseledets subpaces. Using their expression in terms of Oseledets subspaces, we may write

[u,v]=e(u_{1})\cdots e(u_{\ell(\alpha)})\cdot e(v_{1})\cdots e(v_{\ell(\beta)})\cdot e(-u_{\ell(\alpha)})\cdots e(-u_{1})\cdot e(-v_{\ell(\beta)})\cdots e(-v_{1}).

We wish to push the
e(-u_{\ell(\alpha)})
term past the
e(v_{i})
-terms to cancel with
e(u_{\ell(\alpha)})
. Since we have constant commutator relations among the exponentials of Oseledets spaces, we may do so, but accumulate their commutators along the way. We may choose to put them on the left or right whenever we commute, we choose to put them on the left. That is, we write
e(v_{i})e(-u_{\ell(\alpha)})=ge(-u_{\ell(\alpha)})e(v_{i})
, where
g
is a geometric commutator of
e(-v_{i})
and
e(u_{\ell(\alpha)})
determined by the
\rho
-function, which is constant. Recall that
g
is a product of elements from the coarse Lyapunov groups strictly between
\alpha
and
\beta
, which are constant by the assumption of the lemma.

The element
g
itself may be decomposed as a product of Oseledets subspaces, which we may push past
e(v_{1})\cdots e(v_{i-1})
by the same method, accumulating new terms on the left in the process between the coarse weights of
g
and
\beta
. These terms are independent of
x
since we only commute terms coming from exponentials of Oseledets spaces. Since
g
will always take values in coarse weights between
\alpha
and
\beta
, there is a clear induction on
\#\Sigma(\alpha,\beta)
, which terminates since there are only finitely many such coarse weights. We may express
[u,v]
as

[u,v]=e(u_{1})\cdots e(u_{\ell(\alpha)})ge(-u_{\ell(\alpha)})e(v_{1})\cdots e(v_{\ell(\beta)})\cdot e(-u_{\ell(\alpha)-1})\cdots e(-u_{1})\cdot e(-v_{\ell(\beta)})\cdots e(-v_{1}),

where
g
is a product of exponentials of Oseledets space from
\Omega(\alpha,\beta)
, which is still independent of
x
. Using the same procedure as above, we may push all
g
-terms to the far left, and cancel the
e(u_{\ell(\alpha)})
-term with its inverse to obtain the following expression, with
g^{\prime}
independent of
x
:

[u,v]=g^{\prime}e(u_{1})\cdots e(u_{\ell(\alpha)-1})\cdot e(v_{1})\cdots e(v_{\ell(\beta)})\cdot e(-u_{\ell(\alpha)-1})\cdots e(-u_{1})\cdot e(-v_{\ell(\beta)})\cdots e(-v_{1}).

We now repeat this process until we have canceled each
e(u_{i})
-term which then further allows for the cancelling of all
e(v_{j})
-terms, leaving only a product exponentials of Oseledets spaces from
\Omega(\alpha,\beta)
, which is independent of
x
.

We have now reduced
[u,v]
to a product of exponentials of Oseledets spaces of
\gamma
, where
\gamma\in\Sigma(\alpha,\beta)
. To deduce that
\rho^{\alpha,\beta}(u,v,x)
is independent of
x
, we write
\Sigma(\alpha,\beta)
in a circular ordering. Then push all of the exponential terms from each the Oseledets subspace of the first coarse exponent to the left. Since the commutators
\rho^{\alpha,\beta}(e(u_{i}),e(v_{j}))
are determined by the functions
\hat{\rho}
, which are assumed to be constant, this is possible, accumulating their commutators on the right. The corresponding reduction yields an element, written in circular ordering, which is independent of
x
. Uniqueness follows from Lemma
11.13
.
∎

12.2.
Setting up the inductions

We prove Theorem
12.1
using Lemma
12.4
by showing each
\hat{\rho}^{c_{i}^{\alpha}\alpha,c_{j}^{\beta}\beta}_{c_{m}^{\gamma}\gamma}
is constant. We use use three inductions. The outermost induction is on
\#\Sigma(\alpha,\beta)
, we call this Induction I.
In each step of the induction, we will show for Lyapunov exponents
c_{i}^{\alpha}\alpha
and
c_{j}^{\beta}\beta
,

(12.2)

if
\hat{\rho}^{c_{i}^{\alpha}\alpha,c_{j}^{\beta}\beta}_{c_{m}^{\gamma}\gamma}\not\equiv 0
, then
c_{m}^{\gamma}\gamma=\sigma\,c_{i}^{\alpha}\alpha+\tau\,c_{j}^{\beta}\beta
for some
\sigma,\tau\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
, and

(12.3)

if
c_{m}^{\gamma}\gamma=\sigma\,c_{i}^{\alpha}\alpha+\tau\,c_{j}^{\beta}\beta
, then
\hat{\rho}^{c_{i}^{\alpha}\alpha,c_{j}^{\beta}\beta}_{c_{m}^{\gamma}\gamma}(u,v,x)
is a polynomial which is
\sigma
-homogeneous in
u
,

\tau
-homogeneous in
v
and independent of
x
.

We first state a key consequence of the induction. Let
\mathcal{P}_{|\alpha,\beta|}
denote the group freely generated by the groups
N^{\gamma}
,
\gamma\in\Sigma(\alpha,\beta)\cup\left\{\alpha,\beta\right\}
.

Lemma 12.5
.

If (
12.2
) and (
12.3
) hold for all linearly independent
\alpha,\beta
such that
\#\Sigma(\alpha,\beta)\leq n
, then for any such
\alpha,\beta
,

•

the action of
\mathcal{P}_{|\alpha,\beta|}
factors through the action of a nilpotent Lie group
N^{|\alpha,\beta|}
,

•

\operatorname{Lie}(N^{|\alpha,\beta|})=\bigoplus_{\gamma\in\Sigma(\alpha,\beta)\cup\left\{\alpha,\beta\right\}}\operatorname{Lie}(N^{\gamma})
, and

•

the family of automorphisms
a_{*}
defined in
(HA-3)
descend to an automorphism of
N^{|\alpha,\beta|}
.

Proof.

Write
\Sigma(\alpha,\beta)\cup\left\{\alpha,\beta\right\}=\left\{\alpha=\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\dots,\gamma_{r}=\beta\right\}
in the induced circular ordering. Let
\mathcal{G}
denote the factor of the group
\mathcal{P}_{\left|\alpha,\beta\right|}
modulo the commutator relations (
11.3
). By (
12.3
) and Lemma
12.4
, they are independent of
x
. We first claim that every
\rho\in\mathcal{G}
can be written as

(12.4)

u_{1}*\dots*u_{r},

where each
u_{i}\in N^{\gamma_{i}}
is unique. Indeed, any
\rho\in\mathcal{G}
can be written as
\rho=v_{1}*\dots*v_{k}
(where each
v_{i}\in N^{\beta_{n_{i}}}
).

We may begin by pushing all of the terms from the
\beta_{1}
component to the left. We do this by looking at the first term to appear with
\beta_{1}
. Each time we pass it through, we may accumulate some
[u^{\beta_{1}},v^{\beta_{j}}]
which may be rewritten as
\rho(u^{\beta_{1}},v^{\beta_{j}})
, having no
\beta_{1}
terms, since we have quotiented by the commutator relations (
11.3
). So we have shown that in
\mathcal{G}
,
\rho
is equal to
u_{1}*\rho^{\prime}
, where
\rho^{\prime}
consists only of terms without
\beta_{1}
, and
u_{1}\in N^{\beta_{1}}
.

We now proceed inductively. We may in the same way push all
\beta_{2}
terms to the left. Notice now that each time we pass through, the “commutator”
\rho(u^{\beta_{2}},v^{\beta_{j}})
,
j\geq 3
has no
\beta_{1}
or
\beta_{2}
terms. Iterating this process yields the desired presentation of
\rho
.

Thus, every element of
\mathcal{G}
has a unique presentation of the form (
12.4
), where the uniqueness follows from Lemma
11.13
. The map which assigns an element
\rho
to such a presentation gives a an injective map from
\mathcal{G}
to
\prod N^{\beta_{i}}
(but the map may not be a homomorphism of groups). By Lemma
6.3
, it will be continuous once its lift to
\mathcal{P}_{\left|\alpha,\beta\right|}
is continuous. In each combinatorial cell
C_{\overline{\beta}}
, the map is given by composition of the group multiplications in each
N^{\beta_{i}}
and the functions
\rho^{\alpha,\beta}(\cdot,\cdot)
evaluated on cell coordinates, which are continuous. Therefore, the lift is continuous, so the map from
\mathcal{G}
is continuous.

Therefore, there is an injective continuous map from
\mathcal{G}
to a finite-dimensional space, and
\mathcal{G}
is a Lie group by Corollary
6.8
. Fix
a
which contracts every
\beta_{i}
. The fact that
\mathcal{G}
is nilpotent follows from the fact that it has a contracting automorphism.
∎

Each step of the outer induction on
\#\Sigma(\alpha,\beta)
will be proved using two further inductions. We introduce a partial order on
\left\{(i,j):1\leq i\leq\ell(\alpha),1\leq j\leq\ell(\beta)\right\}
by saying that
(i_{1},j_{1})\preceq(i_{2},j_{2})
if and only if
i_{1}\leq i_{2}
and
j_{1}\leq j_{2}
. The second induction will utilize this partial order: we will show (
12.2
) and (
12.3
) for a pair
c_{i}^{\alpha}\alpha
and
c_{j}^{\beta}\beta
assuming that we have concluded it for all choices of
c_{m}^{\gamma}\gamma
and all
c_{i^{\prime}}^{\alpha}\alpha
,
c_{j^{\prime}}^{\beta}\beta
such that
(i,j)\preceq(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})
and
(i,j)\not=(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})
. The base of this induction will then be
(\ell(\alpha),\ell(\beta))
, the unique maximal element. It is clear from the structure of the partial order that such an induction will exhaust all choices of
(i,j)
. We call this induction Induction II.

Given
c_{i}^{\alpha}\alpha
and
c_{j}^{\beta}\beta
, let

(12.5)

[c_{i}^{\alpha}\alpha,c_{j}^{\alpha}\beta]=\left\{c_{m}^{\gamma}\gamma:\hat{\rho}^{c_{i}^{\alpha}\alpha,c_{j}^{\beta}\beta}_{c_{m}^{\gamma}\gamma}\not\equiv 0\right\}\mbox{ and }\Omega_{l}=\left\{\sigma\,c_{i}^{\alpha}\alpha+\tau\,c_{j}^{\beta}\beta:\sigma+\tau=l,\;\sigma,\tau>0\right\}\cap\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Omega(\alpha,\beta)\color[rgb]{0,0,0}.

Then there are finitely many values
l_{0}<l_{1}<\dots<l_{m}
such that

\Omega(\alpha,\beta)=\bigcup_{p=0}^{m}\Omega_{l_{p}}.

Given a subset
S
of Lyapunov exponents, and a weight
c_{i}^{\alpha}\alpha\in\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Omega\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
, let

[c_{i}^{\alpha}\alpha,S]=\left\{c_{m}^{\gamma}\gamma:\rho^{c_{i}^{\alpha}\alpha,c_{j}^{\beta}\beta}_{c_{m}^{\gamma}\gamma}\not\equiv 0\mbox{ for some }c_{j}^{\beta}\beta\in S\right\}.

Let
\mathcal{P}_{(\alpha,\beta)}
,
\mathcal{P}_{|\alpha,\beta)}
and
\mathcal{P}_{(\alpha,\beta|}
denote the groups freely generated by the coarse weights of
\Sigma(\alpha,\beta)
,
\Sigma(\alpha,\beta)\cup\left\{\alpha\right\}
and
\Sigma(\alpha,\beta)\cup\left\{\beta\right\}
, respectively. By the induction hypothesis, the action of each group factors through Lie groups which we denote by
N^{(\alpha,\beta)}
,
N^{|\alpha,\beta)}
and
N^{(\alpha,\beta|}
, respectively. Let
F_{l}=\bigoplus_{c_{m}^{\gamma}\gamma\in\Omega_{l}}E^{c_{m}^{\gamma}\gamma}
, so that
\operatorname{Lie}(N^{(\alpha,\beta)})=\bigoplus_{a=0}^{m}F_{l_{a}}
.

It now
suffices to show Claims (
12.2
) and (
12.3
) for each
\chi\in\Omega_{l_{p}}
. We will do this using a final induction on
p
, starting from
p=0
. We call this induction Induction III. We summarize each induction below, noting that the proof of (
12.2
) and (
12.3
) runs in a lexicographical ordering: for each step of Induction I, we do every step of Induction II, and for each of Induction II, we do every step of Induction III:

•

Induction I:
\#\Sigma(\alpha,\beta)
, base case
\#\Sigma(\alpha,\beta)=0
.

•

Induction II: Partial order on
(c_{i}^{\alpha}\alpha,c_{j}^{\beta}\beta)
, base case maximal element, induction moves downward.

•

Induction III:
c_{m}^{\gamma}\gamma\in\Omega_{l}
, induction on
l
, base case
l=l_{0}
smallest coefficients.

12.3.
Proving the inductive steps

Fix
l
, and given
g\in N^{(\alpha,\beta)}
, write
\log g=\check{g}+\bar{g}+\hat{g}
, where
\check{g}
is the component of
\log g
from the weight spaces of
\Omega_{l^{\prime}}
,
l^{\prime}<l
,
\bar{g}
is the component from the weight spaces of
\Omega_{l}
and
\hat{g}
is the component from the weight spaces of
\Omega_{l^{\prime}}
,
l^{\prime}>l
.

By Induction I and Lemma
12.5
, there are groups
N^{(\alpha,\beta)}
,
N^{|\alpha,\beta)}
and
N^{(\alpha,\beta|}
generated by the coarse weights
\Sigma(\alpha,\beta)
,
\left\{\alpha\right\}\cup\Sigma(\alpha,\beta)
and
\left\{\beta\right\}\cup\Sigma(\alpha,\beta)
. The following important lemma uses these group structures to describe the action of
{N^{\alpha}}
on
N^{(\alpha,\beta)}
.

Lemma 12.6
.

If
u\in E^{c_{i}^{\alpha}\alpha}
, then the conjugation action of
\exp(u)
on
N^{|\alpha,\beta)}
preserves
N^{(\alpha,\beta)}
. Furthermore,
\operatorname{ad}(u)
a nilpotent automorphism such that
\operatorname{ad}(u)(F_{l})\subset F_{l+1}
.

Proof.

That
\operatorname{ad}(u)
is unipotent follows from the fact that
\operatorname{ad}(u)(F_{l})\subset F_{l+1}
, which we now show. Assume
c_{m}^{\gamma}\gamma\in\Omega_{l}
. The Lie group
N^{|\alpha,\beta)}
also carries an automorphism
a_{*}
which expands
E^{c_{i}^{\alpha}\alpha}
by
e^{c_{i}^{\alpha}\alpha(a)}
and
E^{c_{m}^{\gamma}\gamma}
by
e^{c_{m}^{\gamma}\gamma(a)}
. Therefore,
[E^{c_{m}^{\gamma}\gamma},E^{c_{i}^{\alpha}\alpha}]
consists of vectors which are expanded by
e^{c_{m}^{\gamma}\gamma(a)+c_{i}^{\alpha}\alpha(a)}
. Since
a
is arbitrary we conclude that
[E^{c_{m}^{\gamma}\gamma},E^{c_{i}^{\alpha}\alpha}]\subset E^{c_{m}^{\gamma}\gamma+c_{i}^{\alpha}\alpha}
. Since
c_{m}^{\gamma}\gamma\in\Omega_{l}
, the result follows.
∎

A completely symmetric version holds for
u\in E^{c_{j}^{\beta}\beta}
. The following are immediate consequences of Lemma
12.6
, and the Baker-Campbell-Hausdorff formula.

Corollary 12.7
.

If
u\in E^{c_{i}^{\alpha}\alpha}\cup E^{c_{j}^{\beta}\beta}
, and
g=\exp(\check{g}+\bar{g}+\hat{g})\in N^{(\alpha,\beta)}
, let
v=\exp(u)*g*\exp(-u)
. Then:

(1)

v\in N^{(\alpha,\beta)}
,

(2)

\check{v}
is a polynomial of
u
and
\check{g}
,

(3)

\bar{v}
takes the following form:

\bar{v}=p(u,\check{g})+\bar{g}

for some polynomial
p
such that
p(0,\cdot)=p(\cdot,0)=0
, and

(4)

\hat{v}
is a polynomial in
u
,
\check{g}
,
\bar{g},
and
\hat{g}
.

Corollary 12.8
.

If
g_{1},g_{2}\in N^{(\alpha,\beta)}
and we write
g_{3}=g_{1}g_{2}
, then

\check{g}_{3}=p_{1}(\check{g_{1}},\check{g_{2}})\qquad\bar{g}_{3}=\bar{g}_{1}+\bar{g}_{2}+p_{2}(\check{g}_{1},\check{g}_{2})\qquad\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\hat{g}_{3}=p_{3}(\check{g}_{1},\bar{g}_{1},\hat{g}_{1},\check{g}_{2},\bar{g}_{2},\hat{g}_{2}),\color[rgb]{0,0,0}

for some polynomials
p_{1}
,
p_{2}
and
p_{3}
.

Notice that
\rho^{\alpha,\beta}(u,v,x)
is a formal product of elements from the groups
N^{\gamma}
,
\gamma\in\Sigma(\alpha,\beta)
, written in a circular ordering. Therefore, it represents a unique element of
N^{(\alpha,\beta)}
, which we abusively denote with the same notation. Fix
c_{m}^{\gamma}\gamma\in\Omega_{l}
, and define a function

r^{c_{i}^{\alpha}\alpha,c_{j}^{\beta}\beta}_{c_{m}^{\gamma}\gamma}(u,v,x)
to be the
E^{c_{m}^{\gamma}\gamma}
-component of
\log\rho^{\alpha,\beta}(e(u),e(v),x)
.

Corollary 12.9
.

r^{c_{i}^{\alpha}\alpha,c_{j}^{\beta}\beta}_{c_{m}^{\gamma}\gamma}(u,v,x)=\hat{\rho}^{c_{i}^{\alpha}\alpha,c_{j}^{\beta}\beta}_{c_{m}^{\gamma}\gamma}(u,v,x)+p(u,v)
for some polynomial
p:E^{c_{i}^{\alpha}\alpha}\oplus E^{c_{j}^{\beta}\beta}\to E^{c_{m}^{\gamma}\gamma}
independent of
x
.

Proof.

The definitions of
r^{c_{i}^{\alpha}\alpha,c_{j}^{\beta}\beta}_{c_{m}^{\gamma}\gamma}
and
\hat{\rho}^{c_{i}^{\alpha}\alpha,c_{j}^{\beta}\beta}_{c_{m}^{\gamma}\gamma}
are quite similar, the only difference being that
\hat{\rho}^{c_{i}^{\alpha}\alpha,c_{j}^{\beta}\beta}_{c_{m}^{\gamma}\gamma}
uses the
c_{m}^{\gamma}\gamma
-component of
\log\rho^{\alpha,\beta}_{\gamma}
, while
r^{c_{i}^{\alpha}\alpha,c_{j}^{\beta}\beta}_{c_{m}^{\gamma}\gamma}
regards
\rho^{\alpha,\beta}
as an element of
N_{(\alpha,\beta)}
, then takes the
\log
and the
c_{m}^{\gamma}\gamma
-component. Therefore, we wish to compare the standard exponential coordinate system on
N_{(\alpha,\beta)}
and the coordinate system given by
(v_{1},\dots,v_{n})\mapsto e(v_{1})\dots e(v_{n})
, where
v_{i}\in N^{\gamma_{i}}
and the
\gamma_{i}
are listed in a circular ordering. By Corollary
12.8
, the
c_{m}^{\gamma}\gamma
-components will differ only by polynomials that depend on the terms of the commutator coming from
\Omega_{l^{\prime}}
,
l^{\prime}<l
(ie, the
\check{\cdot}
-terms). Since by Induction III, such terms are polynomials,
\hat{\rho}^{c_{i}^{\alpha}\alpha,c_{j}^{\beta}\beta}_{c_{m}^{\gamma}\gamma}
and
r^{c_{i}^{\alpha}\alpha,c_{j}^{\beta}\beta}_{c_{m}^{\gamma}\gamma}
differ by a polynomial in
u
and
v
which is independent of
x
.
∎

Figure
1
gives an example of the structures above. We give a description of the features available for one step of the induction for this particular example. We assume that we are at the stage of the induction to analyze
\hat{\rho}^{c_{2}^{\alpha}\alpha,c_{2}^{\beta}\beta}_{c_{j}^{\gamma_{m}}\gamma_{m}}
. Then
\Omega_{l_{0}}=\left\{c_{1}^{\gamma_{1}}\gamma_{1}\right\}
,
\Omega_{l_{1}}=\left\{c_{1}^{\gamma_{2}}\gamma_{2}\right\}
and
\Omega_{l_{2}}=\left\{c_{2}^{\gamma_{1}}\gamma_{1},c_{2}^{\gamma_{2}}\gamma_{2}\right\}
, since they are the intersections of lines parallel to the one passing through
c_{2}^{\beta}\beta
and
c_{2}^{\alpha}\alpha
. At the first stage of the innermost induction, we would analyze only the function
\hat{\rho}^{c_{2}^{\alpha}\alpha,c_{2}^{\beta}\beta}_{c_{1}^{\gamma_{1}}\gamma_{1}}
. The crucial feature for the base step is that, by Lemma
12.6
, no terms in the Oseledets space
c_{1}^{\gamma_{1}}\gamma_{1}
can appear by commuting the
c_{i}^{\gamma_{j}}\gamma_{j}
with another weight in the figure. The second induction is necessary, due to the fact that
E^{c_{2}^{\alpha}\alpha}
may only be a
vector
subspace of
\operatorname{Lie}(N^{\alpha})
and not be a subalgebra, and the algebraic properties of this subspace will be crucial in understanding the dependence of
\hat{\rho}^{c_{2}^{\alpha}\alpha,c_{2}^{\beta}\beta}_{c_{1}^{\gamma_{1}}\gamma_{1}}(u,v,x)
on
u
. Luckily, some algebraic features remain. If
u_{1},u_{2}\in E^{c_{2}^{\alpha}\alpha}
, we may write
e(u_{1}+u_{2})=e(u_{1})e(u_{2})\cdot g
for some
g\in N^{\alpha}
. In fact, such a
g
must lie in
\exp(E^{c_{3}^{\alpha}\alpha}\oplus E^{c_{4}^{\alpha}\alpha})
. By the second induction on the pairs
c_{i}^{\alpha}\alpha
and
c_{j}^{\beta}\beta
, we know that this additional term
g
will have polynomial relations with
c_{2}^{\beta}\beta
and has polynomial relations with each
c_{j}^{\gamma_{m}}\gamma_{m}
by the first induction on
\#\Sigma(\alpha,\beta)
. This allows the analysis to go through.

In the next step of the induction on the
\Omega_{l_{i}}
,
c_{1}^{\gamma_{2}}\gamma_{2}
terms
may
appear when commuting the
c_{1}^{\gamma_{1}}\gamma_{1}
terms with a multiple of
\alpha
or
\beta
(which will be needed in Lemma
12.10
), but for this example, this is the only Lyapunov exponent
strictly
between
\alpha
and
\beta
with this property (by considering Figure
1
and Lemma
12.6
). This can and will appear when analyzing how the function
\hat{\rho}^{c_{2}^{\alpha}\alpha,c_{2}^{\beta},\beta}_{c_{1}^{\gamma_{2}}\gamma_{2}}(u,v,x)
depends on
u
. Such terms will contribute polynomials by the induction hypothesis, leading to the final polynomial form.

c_{1}^{\alpha}\alpha
c_{2}^{\alpha}\alpha
c_{4}^{\alpha}\alpha
c_{3}^{\alpha}\alpha
c_{1}^{\beta}\beta
c_{2}^{\beta}\beta
c_{1}^{\gamma_{1}}\gamma_{1}
c_{2}^{\gamma_{1}}\gamma_{1}
c_{1}^{\gamma_{2}}\gamma_{2}
c_{2}^{\gamma_{2}}\gamma_{2}
\beta
\alpha
\gamma_{1}
\gamma_{2}

Figure 1.
Lyapunov exponents in
\Sigma(\alpha,\beta)

We now return to the formal proof, assuming the induction hypotheses. Assume Claims (
12.2
) and (
12.3
) hold for
c_{m}^{\gamma}\gamma\in\Omega_{l_{q}}
,
q<p
.

Fix
v\in E^{c_{j}^{\beta}\beta}
and let
\varphi(u,x)=\hat{\rho}^{c_{i}^{\alpha}\alpha,c_{j}^{\beta}\beta}_{c_{m}^{\gamma}\gamma}(u,v,x)
. Notice that when
a\in\ker\beta
and
c_{m}^{\gamma}\gamma=\sigma\,c_{i}^{\alpha}\alpha+\tau\,c_{j}^{\beta}\beta
, (
11.1
),
(HA-3)
and the definition of
\hat{\rho}^{c_{i}^{\alpha}\alpha,c_{j}^{\beta}\beta}_{c_{m}^{\gamma}\gamma}
implies that

(12.6)

\varphi(u,x)=e^{-\sigma c_{i}^{\alpha}\alpha(a)}\varphi(e^{c_{i}^{\alpha}\alpha(a)}u,a\cdot x).

We are now ready to establish the key lemma which gives a cocycle-like property to the function
\varphi
. While the proof requires checking some complicated details, the following lemma follows from two simple ideas: splitting a commutator into a sum of two commutators requires a conjugation and reordering, and with careful bookkeeping, the reordering and conjugation can be shown to contribute polynomial terms only. By the Baker-Campbell-Hausdorff formula, for homogeneous systems where the functions
\varphi
are compositions of multiplication in a nilpotent Lie group, such polynomials will be nonvanishing unless
\sigma=\tau=1
, and the cocycle equation without them will not hold. We assume that the induction hypotheses hold.

Lemma 12.10
.

\varphi(u_{1}+u_{2},x)=\varphi(u_{1},x)+\varphi(u_{2},e(u_{1})x)+p(u_{1},u_{2})
for some polynomial
p:E^{c_{i}^{\alpha}\alpha}\times E^{c_{i}^{\alpha}\alpha}\to E^{c_{m}^{\gamma}\gamma}
such that
p(0,\cdot)\equiv 0
and
p(\cdot,0)\equiv 0
.

Proof.

We assume that
c_{m}^{\gamma}\gamma\in\Omega_{l_{p}}
. Recall that
\varphi(u,x)={\hat{\rho}^{c_{i}^{\alpha}\alpha,c_{j}^{\beta}\beta}_{c_{m}^{\gamma}\gamma}}(u,v,x)
is the
c_{m}^{\gamma}\gamma
-component of the unique path
\rho^{\alpha,\beta}_{\gamma}(u,v,x)
, written in circular ordering of the coarse weights in
\Sigma(\alpha,\beta)
, which connects
[e(u),e(v)]\cdot x
and
x
. Given
u_{1},u_{2}\in E^{c_{i}\alpha}
, there exists
q(u_{1},u_{2})\in\bigoplus_{i^{\prime}>i}E^{c_{i^{\prime}}^{\alpha}\alpha}
such that
e(u_{1}+u_{2})=e(q(u_{1},u_{2}))e(u_{2})e(u_{1})
. Since
N^{\alpha}
is nilpotent,
q
is a polynomial in
u_{1}
and
u_{2}
. Notice that using only the free product relations, we get that:

\displaystyle[e(u_{1}+u_{2}),e(v)]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e(-v)*e(-u_{1}-u_{2})*e(v)*e(u_{1}+u_{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e(-v)*e(-u_{1}-u_{2})*e(v)*e(q(u_{1},u_{2}))*e(u_{2})*e(u_{1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e(-v)*e(-u_{1}-u_{2})*e(v)*e(q(u_{1},u_{2}))*e(u_{2})*\big(e(-v)*e(u_{1})*e(v)\big)

\displaystyle\qquad*\;\big(e(-v)*e(-u_{1})*e(v))*e(u_{1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e(-v)*e(-u_{1})*e(-u_{2})*e(-q(u_{1},u_{2}))*e(v)*e(q(u_{1},u_{2}))*e(u_{2})

\displaystyle\qquad*\;\big(e(-v)*e(u_{1})*e(v)\big)*[e(u_{1}),e(v)]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e(-v)*e(-u_{1})*e(-u_{2})*e(-q(u_{1},u_{2}))*e(v)*e(q(u_{1},u_{2}))*e(-v)*e(u_{2})*e(v)

\displaystyle\qquad*\;[e(u_{2}),e(v)]*\big(e(-v)*e(u_{1})*e(v)\big)*[e(u_{1}),e(v)]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\big(e(-v)*e(-u_{1})*e(-u_{2})\big)*[e(-v),e(q(u_{1},u_{2}))]*\big(e(u_{2})*e(u_{1})*e(v)\big)

\displaystyle\qquad*\;\big(e(-v)*e(-u_{1})*e(v)\big)*[e(u_{2}),e(v)]*\big(e(-v)*e(u_{1})*e(v)\big)*[e(u_{1}),e(v)]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big(e(-v)*e(-u_{1})*e(-u_{2})\big)*[e(-v),e(q(u_{1},u_{2}))]*\big(e(u_{2})*e(u_{1})*e(v)\big)}

\displaystyle\qquad*\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\big(e(-v)*e(-u_{1})*e(v)\big)*[e(u_{2}),e(v)]*\big(e(-v)*e(u_{1})*e(v)\big)}*{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0}[e(u_{1}),e(v)]}

The last equality is simply the second-to-last expression rewritten with color-coding. First, consider the red term. Since
q
takes values in
\bigoplus_{i^{\prime}>i}E^{c_{i^{\prime}}^{\alpha}\alpha}
, we know the commutators of
q
with
e(-v)
are polynomial and independent of their basepoint by Induction II. Therefore we may rewrite the red term as
\big(e(-v)*e(-u_{1})*e(-u_{2})\big)*e(\tau_{0}(u_{1},u_{2},v))*\big(e(u_{2})*e(u_{1})*e(v)\big)
, where
\tau_{0}\in\operatorname{Lie}(N^{(\alpha,\beta)})
is independent of
x
, depending polynomially on
u_{1}
,
u_{2}
and
v
. Then by Induction I, we know how each of the conjugating terms act on the term
\tau_{0}
, which must be polynomially. Therefore, the entire first red term is independent of
x
and can be replaced by some
e(\tau(u_{1},u_{2},v))
for some polynomial
\tau
taking values in
\operatorname{Lie}(N^{(\alpha,\beta)})
.

We now turn to the blue terms. Let
y=e(u_{1})\cdot x
, so that
h_{2}(y):=\rho^{\alpha,\beta}(e(u_{2}),e(v),y)\in N^{(\alpha,\beta)}
acts on
y
in the same way as
[e(u_{2}),e(v)]
. By induction, we may apply the conjugation of
h_{2}(y)
by
e(-v)*e(u_{1})*e(v)
as 3 independent ones, which have well-understood forms by Corollary
12.7
. Indeed, applying parts (2) and (3) of the corollary three times shows that the blue terms act on
y
in the same way that
g(y)=\exp(\check{g}+\bar{g}(y)+\hat{g}(y))\in N^{(\alpha,\beta)}
does, where
\check{g}
is polynomial in
u_{2}
and
v
(by applying part (2) and Induction I),
\bar{g}(y)=\bar{h}_{2}(y)+p(u_{2},v)
for some polynomial
p
(by applying part (3)) and
\hat{g}(y)\in\bigoplus_{l^{\prime}>l_{p}}\bigoplus_{c_{m}^{\gamma}\gamma\in\Omega_{l}}E^{c_{m}^{\gamma}\gamma}
is, for now, uncontrolled.

The final green term is straightforward, letting
h_{1}(x)=\rho^{\alpha,\beta}(e(u_{1}),e(v),x)
be the element of
N^{(\alpha,\beta)}
acting on
x
in the same way as
[e(u_{1}),e(v)]
. Then
\check{h}_{1}
is a polynomial in
u_{1}
and
v
independent of
x
by Induction III. Putting the conclusions together yields that
[e(u_{1}+u_{2}),e(v)]
acts on
x
in the same way as:

h(x)=e(\tau(u_{1},u_{2},v))*g(y)*h_{1}(x),

so by Corollary
12.8
,
\check{h}
is a polynomial in
u_{1}
,
u_{2}
and
v
which is independent of
x
, and
\bar{h}(x)=\bar{h}_{1}(e(u_{1})\cdot x)+\bar{h}_{2}(x)+p(u_{1},u_{2},v)
for some polynomial
p
in
u_{1}
,
u_{2}
and
v
(depending only on the group structure of
N^{\alpha,\beta}
and is hence independent of
x
). Therefore, the
\Omega_{l_{p}}
terms have exactly the prescribed form.
∎

Lemma 12.11
.

If
V
and
W
are vector spaces and
f:V\to W
is a continuous function such that
f(0)=0
and

(12.7)

f(v_{1}+v_{2})=f(v_{1})+f(v_{2})+p(v_{1},v_{2})

for some polynomial
p
, then
f
is a polynomial.

Proof.

First, observe that since
f(0+v)=f(0)+f(v)+p(0,v)
, it follows that
p(0,v)=0
. Therefore,
p(v,w)
has no constant terms and
p
is symmetric by (
12.7
). Furthermore, every term of
p
must have degree at least one in
both

v
and
w
since it is symmetric. If
q(v)=p(v,v)
, then every term of
q
has degree at least two. In particular, since each nonzero term of
q
is multiplied by at least
2^{n}\cdot 2^{-2n}
,
\sum 2^{n}q(2^{-n}v)
is summable and the sum converges to a polynomial of the same degree.

From (
12.7
), it follows that
f(v)=2f\left(\frac{1}{2}v\right)+q\left(\frac{1}{2}v\right)
. Inductively, it follows that
f(v)=2^{n}f\left(2^{-n}v\right)+\sum_{i=1}^{n}2^{i-1}q\left(2^{-i}v\right)
. Since the sum on the right hand side converges to a polynomial, it follows that
\lim_{n\to\infty}2^{n}f\left(2^{-n}v\right)
converges to a vector uniformly bounded as
v
varies in a compact set. Therefore, the map
D:v\mapsto\lim_{n\to\infty}2^{n}f\left(2^{-n}v\right)
is well-defined and satisfies

D(v+w)=\lim_{n\to\infty}2^{n}f\left(2^{-n}(v+w)\right)=\lim_{n\to\infty}2^{n}\left(f\left(2^{-n}v\right)+f\left(2^{-n}w\right)+p(2^{-n}v,2^{-n}w)\right)\\
=D(v)+D(w).

Furthermore, since
D(v)=f(v)-\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}2^{i-1}q\left(2^{-i}v\right)
, it follows that
D
is continuous and hence linear. Therefore,
f(v)=D(v)+\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}2^{i-1}q\left(2^{-i}v\right)
, and
f
is a polynomial.
∎

Recall that if we write
c_{m}^{\gamma}\gamma=\sigma\,c_{i}^{\alpha}\alpha+\tau\,c_{j}^{\beta}\beta
, we say that
\sigma
and
\tau
are the Lyapunov coefficients. Since we have assumed integral Lyapunov coefficients (Definition
12.2
), we may assume that either
\sigma\geq 1
or
\tau\geq 1
. We without loss of generality assume that
\sigma\geq 1
.

Corollary 12.12
.

The function
\varphi(u,x)
is a polynomial in
u
, whose coefficients are functions of
x
which are constant along each the sets
\mathcal{F}_{\beta}(m)
defined in Lemma
11.15
.

Proof.

By Lemma
B.1
, it suffices to show the following claim:

Claim 12.12.1.
For every
u,v\in E^{c_{i}^{\alpha}\alpha}
such that
\left|\left|u\right|\right|=1
,
\varphi(tu+v,x)
is a polynomial in
t
whose coefficients are functions of
x
which are constant along the sets
\mathcal{F}_{\beta}(m)
.

Claim 12.12.1 will follow from the following weaker claim:

Claim 12.12.2.

For every
u\in E^{c_{i}^{\alpha}\alpha}
such that
\left|\left|u\right|\right|=1
,
\varphi(tu,x)
is a polynomial in
t
whose coefficients are functions of
x
which are constant along the sets
\mathcal{F}_{\beta}(m)
.

Let us deduce Claim 12.12.1 from Claim 12.12.2. By Lemma
12.10
,

\varphi(tu+v,x)=\varphi(tu,x)+\varphi(v,e(tu)\cdot x)+p(tu,v).

If we can show Claim 12.12.2, then it follows that
\varphi(tu,x)
is a polynomial in
t
whose coefficients are functions of
x
which are constant along the sets
\mathcal{F}_{\beta}(m)
. By Lemma
11.15
the atoms of
\mathcal{F}_{\beta}
are saturated by the leaves of
W^{\alpha}
. Since
\varphi(sv,y)
is a polynomial in
s
whose coefficients are functions of
y
which are constant along atoms of
\mathcal{F}_{\beta}
, it follows that
\varphi(v,e(tu)\cdot x)
is independent of
t
and is constant as
x
moves within the sets
\mathcal{F}_{\beta}(m)
. We have shown that
\varphi(tu+v,x)
is the sum of a polynomial in
t
whose coefficients are functions of
x
which are constant along the sets
\mathcal{F}_{\beta}(m)
, a function of
x
which is constant along the sets
\mathcal{F}_{\beta}(m)
and a polynomial in
t
. Claim 12.12.1 follows.

So we aim to prove Claim 12.12.2. Fix
u\in E^{c_{i}^{\alpha}\alpha}
with
\left|\left|u\right|\right|=1
, and for notational convenience, let
g(t,x)=\varphi(tu,x)
. We claim that for every
x\in X
and Lebesgue almost every
t_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
,
\displaystyle\left.\frac{d}{dt}\right|_{t=t_{1}}g(t,x)
exists. To prove the claim, it suffices to show that
g
is locally Lipschitz. By Lemma
12.10
and (
12.6
),

\displaystyle\left|\left|g(t,x)-g(t_{1},x)\right|\right|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left|\left|g(t-t_{1},e(t_{1}u)\cdot x)+p(t_{1}u,(t-t_{1})u)\right|\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\left|\left|t-t_{1}\right|\right|^{\sigma}\left|\left|g\left(1,a\cdot e(t_{1}u)\cdot x\right)\right|\right|+{\left|\left|p(t_{1}u,(t-t_{1})u)\right|\right|}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}

for a suitable choice of
a\in\ker\beta
. Recall that
\sigma\geq 1
by the integrality assumption and the choice made directly before the statement of Corollary
12.12
. Hence, since
g(1,\cdot)
is bounded,
p
is a polynomial and
\sigma\geq 1
,
g(t,x)
is Lipschitz in any compact neighborhood of
t_{1}
. Therefore, for almost every
t_{1}\in E^{c_{i}^{\alpha}\alpha}
,
g
is differentiable in
t
at
t_{1}
. Therefore, since
g
is differentiable in
t
at
t=0
on a dense set of each orbit of the one-parameter subgroup generated by
u
,
g
is differentiable in
t
at
t=0
on a dense subset of
X
.

By (
12.6
), the set of points for which
g
is differentiable in
t
is invariant under
\mathbb{R}^{k}
. Therefore, if
f(x)
denotes the derivative of
g
in
t
at
t=0
,
f
exists on a dense subset of
X
. We claim that
\left|\left|f(x)\right|\right|\leq B
for some
B\in\mathbb{R}
whenever it exists. Indeed,

\left|f(x)\right|=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\left|g(\varepsilon,x)\right|=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\varepsilon^{\sigma}\left|g(1,a_{\varepsilon}\cdot x)\right|\leq\sup_{y\in X}\left|g(1,y)\right|

where
a_{\varepsilon}\in\ker\beta
is chosen appropriately (using
(
12.6
)), since
\sigma\geq 1
.
Notice that if
a\in\ker\beta
, then again by (
12.6
) and the chain rule,

f(a\cdot x)=e^{(\sigma-1)c_{i}^{\alpha}\alpha(a)}f(x).

Therefore, either
\sigma=1
or
f\equiv 0
, since otherwise one may apply an element
a\in\ker\beta
with
\alpha(a)
arbitrarily large to contradict the boundedness of
f
. Since
\sigma=1
or
f\equiv 0
,
f
is constant along
\ker\beta
orbits.

We claim that
f
is also constant along
W^{\alpha}
leaves whenever it exists. Assume it is not identically 0, otherwise the claim follows immediately. In this case
\sigma=1
, and
g
must be a cocycle over the
e(tu)
-action by comparing Lemma
12.10
and (
12.6
). By
(HA-9)
, there exists an SRB-like measure
\mu
which is invariant under the
\ker\beta
-action and has absolutely continuous disintegrations along
N^{\alpha}
-leaves. By standard Hopf argument, it follows that each ergodic component for the ergodic decomposition of
\mu
with respect to the
\ker\beta
-action is also absolutely continuous along
N^{\alpha}
-leaves.

Hence, for
\mu
-almost every point
x
,
f
is constant at Lebesgue-almost every point of
W^{\alpha}
(since it is a
\ker\beta
-invariant function, and defined at Lebesgue almost every point of
every

W^{\alpha}
leaf). Since the
e(tu)
-orbit foliation is a smooth subfoliation of
N^{\alpha}
, it follows that the disintegration of
\mu
is absolutely continuous on almost every such orbit. Hence
f
is constant on almost every
e(tu)
-orbit. Since
f
is the derivative of
g
, and
g
is a cocycle over the
e(tu)
-flow, it follows that

g(t,x)=\int_{0}^{t}f(e(ru)\cdot x)\,dr=v_{x}t

where
v_{x}\in E^{c_{m}^{\gamma}\gamma}
is the common value of
f
at almost every point of
W^{\alpha}(x)
. Then
g
is linear on a dense set of
W^{\alpha}
leaves, and has derivative invariant under
\ker\beta
. Since
g
is continuous, it follows that
g(t,x)=v_{x}t
, where
v_{x}
is a derivative which depends only on the atom of
\mathcal{F}_{\beta}
. This concludes the case when
\sigma=1
.

We return to the general case (either
\sigma=1
or
f\equiv 0
). Let
v_{x}
denote the common value for
f
along
W^{\alpha}(x)
and
\ker\beta\cdot x
. Fix
t_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
such that
f(e(t_{1}u)\cdot x)
exists (recall that the collection of such
t_{1}
has full Lebesgue measure). Then by Lemma
12.10

(12.8)

\left.\frac{d}{dt}\right|_{t=t_{1}}g(t,x)=\left.\dfrac{d}{dt}\right|_{t=0}\varphi((t+t_{1})u,x)\\
=\left.\dfrac{d}{dt}\right|_{t=0}\Big(\varphi(t_{1}u,x)+\varphi(tu,e(t_{1}u)\cdot x)+p(t_{1}u,tu)\Big)=f(e(t_{1}u)\cdot x)=v_{x}+q(t_{1})

where
q
is some polynomial independent of
x
. Since we know the initial condition
g(0,x)=0
, one may integrate
v_{x}+q
to a get a polynomial form for
g
at each
x
. Furthermore, since
v_{x}
does not vary as
x
moves along
\ker\beta
, the coefficients of the polynomial are constant along the atoms of
\mathcal{F}_{\beta}
. This shows Claim 12.12.2, and finishes the proof.
∎

Proof of the inductive step (
12.2
) and (
12.3
).

Corollary
12.12
implies that for fixed
v
, the function
\hat{\rho}^{c_{i}^{\alpha}\alpha,c_{j}^{\beta}\beta}_{c_{m}^{\gamma}\gamma}(u,v,x)
is a polynomial in
u
, whose coefficients are functions of the atoms of
\mathcal{F}_{\beta}
. It is not difficult to see that an analogous version of Lemma
12.10
holds when fixing
u
and varying
v
. Indeed, if we define
\psi(v,x)=\hat{\rho}^{c_{i}^{\alpha}\alpha,c_{j}^{\beta}\beta}_{c_{m}^{\gamma}\gamma}(u,v,x)
, then a nearly identical proof shows that

(12.9)

\psi(v_{1}+v_{2},x)=\psi(v_{1},x)+\psi(v_{2},e(v_{1})*e(u)\cdot x)+p(u,v_{1},v_{2})

for some polynomial
p
. Furthermore, since we have already shown that
\hat{\rho}^{c_{i}^{\alpha}\alpha,c_{j}^{\beta}\beta}_{c_{m}^{\gamma}\gamma}(u,v,x)
is a polynomial in
u
, it follows that the following equation for
\psi
analogous to (
12.6
) for
\varphi
holds for all
a\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
, not just
a\in\ker\alpha
:

(12.10)

\psi(v,x)=e^{-\tau c_{j}^{\beta}\beta(a)}\psi(e^{c_{i}^{\beta}\beta(a)}v,a\cdot x).

Thus,
\psi
is invariant under
\ker\beta
, and hence constant on each atom of
\mathcal{F}_{\beta}
.

Since the leaves of
W^{\alpha}
and
W^{\beta}
are contained in the atoms of
\mathcal{F}_{\beta}
by Lemma
11.15
, the dependence of
\psi
on
x
does not affect (
12.9
). Therefore, on each atom of
\mathcal{F}_{\beta}
, the map
\psi
satisfies the assumption of Lemma
12.11
and on each atom of
\mathcal{F}_{\beta}
, the function
\hat{\rho}^{c_{i}^{\alpha}\alpha,c_{j}^{\beta}\beta}_{c_{m}^{\gamma}\gamma}(u,v,x)
is a polynomial in
u
and
v
, with coefficients depending only on the atom.

We must show that the polynomials are independent of
x
. Notice that
\hat{\rho}^{c_{i}^{\alpha}\alpha,c_{j}^{\beta}\beta}_{c_{m}^{\gamma}\gamma}(u,v,x)
is a family of polynomials whose coefficients depend on
x
, so (
11.1
) implies that the polynomial is
\sigma
-homogeneous in
u
and
\tau
-homogeneous in
v
, since otherwise the coefficients would grow to
\infty
by applying contracting or expanding elements of
\mathbb{R}^{k}
(so, in particular,
\sigma,\tau\in\mathbb{Z}
and (
12.2
) holds). Therefore, the polynomial is unchanged as
x
moves along its
\mathbb{R}^{k}
-orbit by (
11.1
). Since there is a dense
\mathbb{R}^{k}
-orbit and
\hat{\rho}^{c_{i}^{\alpha}\alpha,c_{j}^{\beta}\beta}_{c_{m}^{\gamma}\gamma}
is continuous in all variables, it follows that it is independent of
x
. Hence (
12.3
) holds. ∎

13.
Partial homogeneity implies homogeneity

The goal of this section is to produce homogeneous structures related to the partial homogeneous structures coming from the
\mathbb{R}^{k}
- and
N^{\alpha}
-actions. The arguments and approach expand those of
[
72
, Section 14]
in several ways. While the overall scheme is similar, several new obstacles appear due to the presence of the compact group
K
as well the coarse Lyapunov foliations being parameterized by general nilpotent Lie groups (rather than copies of
\mathbb{R}
). In particular, we build group actions to describe homogeneous spaces and use Corollary
6.10
to produce Lie structures on them.

We carefully piece together such homogeneous structures to build one on
X
. In particular, we use specific, computable relations between the flows of negatively proportional weights provided by the classification in Lemma
11.17
. We will see that such relations (which we call
symplectic
) yield a canonical presentation of paths, but only for a dense set of paths containing an open neighborhood of the identity in
\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{\left\{\alpha,-\alpha\right\}}
(recall the definition of
\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{\left\{\alpha,-\alpha\right\}}
in Definition
6.5
).

Our approach is to find canonical presentations for words in
\hat{\mathcal{P}}
using only commutator relations and symplectic relations which we assume are constant and well-defined. By fixing a regular element, we will be able to use such relations to rearrange the terms in an open set of words to write them using only stable legs, then only unstable legs, then the action (Proposition
13.11
). This will imply that the quotient group of
\hat{\mathcal{P}}
by the commutator relations and symplectic relations is locally compact, which allows us to use the structure of locally compact groups (Corollary
6.10
). The core of the approach is Lemma
13.9
, which gives the ability to commute stable and unstable paths.

In the end we will have shown that if an ideal factor has constant
\rho
-functions, it is conjugate to a homogeneous action. In particular, this holds for any maximal factor, which sets up the induction on factoring out by a chain of ideals in Section
12
. Recall Definition
5.3
.

Definition 13.1
.

We say that a HAPHA has
constant pairwise cycle structure
if

(CPCS-1)

for each pair of nonproportional
\alpha,\beta\in\Delta
,
\gamma\in\Sigma(\alpha,\beta)
and fixed
u\in N^{\alpha}
and
v\in N^{\beta}
,
\rho^{\alpha,\beta}_{\gamma}(u,v,y)
(see Definition
11.12
) is independent of
y\in X
, and

(CPCS-2)

for each
\alpha\in\Delta
such that
-\alpha\in\Delta
, the action of
\mathcal{P}_{\left\{\alpha,-\alpha\right\}}
factors through a Lie group action on
X
.

In applications, the conditions above are deduced from the genuinely higher-rank assumption
(HA-8)
and the SRB measure assumption
(HA-9)
(indeed, they are consequences of Theorem
12.1
and Lemma
11.17
, respectively). However, we do not need the genuinely higher-rank assumptions to conclude the main goal of this section:

Theorem 13.2
.

If
\mathbb{R}^{k}\times K\curvearrowright X
is a HAPHA with constant pairwise cycle structure, then the action is topologically conjugate to a translation action on a homogeneous space
G/\Gamma
, with
\Gamma
discrete.

13.1.
Stable-unstable-neutral presentations

We let
\rho^{\alpha,\beta}_{\gamma}(u,v)
denote the common value of
\rho^{\alpha,\beta}_{\gamma}(u,v,x)
for
\alpha,\beta\in\Delta
,
\gamma\in\Sigma(\alpha,\beta)
. This is guaranteed to be independent of
x
since the action has constant pairwise cycle structure.
Let
\mathcal{P}\;(=\mathcal{P}_{\Delta})
be the group freely generated by the groups
N^{\alpha}
. Let
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
be the smallest closed normal subgroup containing all cycles of the form

•

[u,v]*\rho^{\alpha,\beta}(u,v)
for
u\in N^{\alpha}
and
v\in N^{\beta}
as described in Definition
11.12
and

•

any element of
\mathcal{P}_{\left\{\alpha,-\alpha\right\}}
which factors through the identity of the Lie group action provided by
(CPCS-2)
(ie,
\ker(\mathcal{P}_{\left\{\alpha,-\alpha\right\}}\to G_{\alpha})
, where
G_{\alpha}
is as in Lemma
11.17
).

Since such cycles are cycles at every point by assumption,
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{C}(x):=\operatorname{Stab}_{\mathcal{P}}(x)
and
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
is normal. Consider the quotient group
\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{P}/\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
.

Fix a regular element
a_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
. The goal of this subsection is to show that any
\rho\in\mathcal{G}
can be reduced (via the relations in
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
) to some
\rho_{+}*\rho_{-}*\rho_{0}
, with
\rho_{+}
having only terms from
N^{\chi}
with
\chi\in\Delta^{+}(a_{0})
,
\rho_{-}
having only terms from
N^{\chi}
with
\chi\in\Delta^{-}(a_{0})
, and
\rho_{0}
being a product of elements of
\mathbb{R}^{k}\times K
generated by symplectic pairs (see Lemma
11.17
). Rather, we will show this for the group obtained by taking the semidirect product of the
\mathbb{R}^{k}\times K
with
\mathcal{G}
see Proposition
13.11
. We begin by identifying well-behaved subgroups of
\mathcal{G}
. Given a subset
\Xi\subset\Delta
, let
\mathcal{G}_{\Xi}
denote the subgroup of
\mathcal{G}
generated by the subgroups
N^{\chi}
, as
\chi
ranges over
\Xi
. We say that
\Xi
is
stable
if
\Xi\subset\Delta^{-}(a)
for some
a\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
. We say that it is
closed
if for any
\alpha,\beta\in\Xi
,
\Sigma(\alpha,\beta)\subset\Xi
.

Lemma 13.3
.

If
\Xi
is a stable, closed collection of coarse weights, then
\mathcal{G}_{\Xi}
is a nilpotent Lie group.

Proof.

The lemma follows as in the proof of Lemma
12.5
. Indeed, the proof works verbatim after restricting the coarse weights
\chi\in\Xi
to a generic
\mathbb{R}^{2}\subset\mathbb{R}^{k}
. Then one uses the circular ordering to find unique presentations of
\mathcal{G}_{\Xi}
. See also
[
72
]
, Section 5.2 and Lemma 17.5.
∎

Let
\chi\in\Delta
be a coarse weight such that
-\chi\in\Delta
, and
\beta\in\Delta
be any linearly independent coarse weight. Let
\Xi=\left\{t\beta+s\chi:t\geq 0,s\in\mathbb{R}\right\}\cap\Delta
, and
\Xi^{\prime}=\left\{t\beta+s\chi:t>0,s\in\mathbb{R}\right\}\cap\Delta=\Xi\setminus\left\{\chi,-\chi\right\}
.

Proposition 13.4
.

If
\Xi
is as above and
\rho\in\mathcal{G}_{\Xi}
is any element, then
\rho=\rho_{\chi}*\rho_{\Xi^{\prime}}
, where
\rho_{\chi}\in\mathcal{G}_{\left\{\chi,-\chi\right\}}
, and
\rho_{\Xi^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{G}_{\Xi^{\prime}}
. Furthermore, such a decomposition is unique.

Proof.

The proof technique is the same as that of Lemma
12.5
. Using constancy of commutator relations, we may push any elements of
N^{\pm\chi}
to the left, accumulating elements of
\mathcal{G}_{\Xi^{\prime}}
as the commutator on the right.

To see uniquness, suppose that
\rho_{\chi}*\rho_{\Xi^{\prime}}=\rho_{\chi}^{\prime}*\rho_{\Xi^{\prime}}^{\prime}
. Then
(\rho_{\chi}^{\prime})^{-1}*\rho_{\chi}=\rho_{\Xi^{\prime}}^{\prime}*\rho_{\Xi^{\prime}}^{-1}
. But
\mathcal{G}_{\Xi^{\prime}}
is a a subgroup of
\mathcal{G}_{\Xi}
and it is clear that
\mathcal{G}_{\Xi^{\prime}}\cap\mathcal{G}_{\left\{\chi,-\chi\right\}}=\left\{e\right\}
. Therefore,
\rho_{\chi}^{\prime}=\rho_{\chi}
and
\rho_{\Xi^{\prime}}=\rho_{\Xi^{\prime}}^{\prime}
, and the decomposition is unique.
∎

Corollary 13.5
.

If
\Xi
is as above,
\mathcal{G}_{\Xi}
is a Lie group. Furthermore,
\mathcal{G}_{\Xi}
has the semidirect product structure
\mathcal{G}_{\left\{\chi,-\chi\right\}}\ltimes\mathcal{G}_{\Xi^{\prime}}
, with
\mathcal{G}_{\Xi^{\prime}}
a nilpotent group.

Proof.

Notice that in the proof of Proposition
13.4
, we get a unique expression by moving the elements of
\mathcal{G}_{\left\{\chi,-\chi\right\}}
to the left, and doing so accumulates only the
\mathcal{G}_{\Xi^{\prime}}
terms. Therefore, the decomposition gives
\mathcal{G}_{\Xi}
the structure of a semidirect product of
\mathcal{G}_{\left\{\chi,-\chi\right\}}
and
\mathcal{G}_{\Xi^{\prime}}
. These groups are Lie by
(CPCS-2)
and Lemma
13.3
, respectively. The action of
\mathcal{G}_{\left\{\chi,-\chi\right\}}
on
\mathcal{G}_{\Xi^{\prime}}
is continuous since the action of its generating subgroups corresponding to
\chi
and
-\chi
are given by commutators, which are continuous. Therefore,
\mathcal{G}_{\Xi}
is the semidirect product of the Lie group
\mathcal{G}_{\left\{\chi,-\chi\right\}}
with the Lie group
\mathcal{G}_{\Xi^{\prime}}
, with a continuous representation, and is hence a Lie group. ∎

The crucial tool in showing that
\mathcal{P}/\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
is Lie is to show that it is locally Euclidean. To that end, the crucial result is Lemma
13.9
.
Fix a regular element
a_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
, then define

\mathcal{G}_{+}=\mathcal{G}_{\Delta^{+}(a_{0})}
and
\mathcal{G}_{-}=\mathcal{G}_{\Delta^{-}(a_{0})}
. Note that
\mathcal{G}_{\pm}
are nilpotent Lie groups by Lemma
13.3
.
Let
\mathcal{D}_{\chi}
be the subgroup of
\mathcal{G}_{\{\chi,-\chi\}}
which was denoted by
G_{0,\chi}
in Lemma
11.17
. Let
\mathcal{D}\subset\mathcal{G}
be the group freely generated by all such
\mathcal{D}_{\chi}
. Note that the action of each element of
\mathcal{D}
commutes with the
\mathbb{R}^{k}
action by construction. Furthermore, the action of
\mathcal{D}
is not obviously faithful, and may fail to be, as is the case for the Weyl chamber flow on
SL(3,\mathbb{R})
where there are 3 symplectic pairs of weights, each generating one-parameter subgroups of
\operatorname{Diag}\cong\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

Lemma 13.6
.

Suppose the
\mathbb{R}^{k}
orbit of
x_{0}
is dense. Then
if
d\in\mathcal{D}
is a cycle at
x_{0}
, then
d
is a cycle everywhere.

Proof.

We know that the action of
\mathcal{D}
commutes with the
\mathbb{R}^{k}
-action. Then if
d
is a cycle at
x_{0}
,
d
is a cycle at any point in
\mathbb{R}^{k}\cdot x_{0}
, hence everywhere as the
\mathbb{R}^{k}
orbit of
x_{0}
is dense.
∎

Lemma 13.7
.

If
u\in N^{\beta}\subset\mathcal{G}
and
g\in\mathcal{D}\subset\mathcal{G}
, then
gug^{-1}\in N^{\beta}\subset\mathcal{G}
.

Proof.

It suffices to show this when
g\in\mathcal{D}_{\chi}
, since elements of
\mathcal{D}
are always products of such elements. Notice that
\mathcal{D}_{\chi}
and
N^{\beta}
are both subgroups of
G_{\Xi}
as in Corollary
13.5
. Furthermore, each
a\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
induces an automorphism of
G_{\Xi}=G_{\left\{\chi,-\chi\right\}}\ltimes G_{\Xi^{\prime}}
. If
X
generates a one-parameter subgroup of
\mathcal{D}_{\chi}
and
Y
generates a one-parameter subgroup of
N^{\beta}
tangent to a Lyapunov subspace, then
a_{*}X=X
and
a_{*}Y=e^{c_{i}\beta(a)}Y
for some
c_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
. Since
a_{*}
is an automorphism
a_{*}[X,Y]=e^{c_{i}\beta(a)}[X,Y]
. Therefore,
[X,Y]
belongs to the same eigenspace as
Y
and is an element of
\operatorname{Lie}(N^{\beta})
. Therefore,
\operatorname{Lie}(\mathcal{D}_{\chi})
normalizes
\operatorname{Lie}(N^{\beta})
, and hence
D_{\chi}
normalizes
N^{\beta}
, as claimed.
∎

Denote by
\mathcal{C}_{D}
the group of cycles in
\mathcal{D}
at a point
x_{0}
with a dense
\mathbb{R}^{k}
-orbit (such a point exists by
(HA-1)
), and set
G=\mathcal{G}/\mathcal{C}_{D}
and
D=\mathcal{D}/\mathcal{C}_{D}
. Notice that
G
and
D
are topological groups since
\mathcal{C}_{D}
is a closed normal subgroup by Lemma
13.6
. Furthermore, let
G_{\pm}
denote the projections of the groups
\mathcal{G}_{\pm}
to
G
. The following lemma is immediate from Lemma
13.7
:

Lemma 13.8
.

Let
\rho^{\pm}\in G_{\pm}
(resp.
G_{\pm}(\bar{x})
) and
\rho^{0}\in D
. Then

\rho^{0}\rho^{\pm}(\rho^{0})^{-1}\in G_{\pm}\mbox{ (resp. }G_{\pm}(\bar{x})).

The following is the basic commutation argument. It is an adaptation of Lemma 14.11 of
[
72
]
, with changes to account for the nilpotent groups being multidimensional.

Lemma 13.9
.

For an open set of elements
\rho^{+}\in G_{+}
,
\rho^{-}\in G_{-}
containing
\left\{e\right\}\times\left\{e\right\}
there exist

(\rho^{+})^{\prime}\in G_{+}
,
(\rho^{-})^{\prime}\in G_{-}

and
\rho^{0}\in D
such that

\rho^{+}*\rho^{-}=(\rho^{-})^{\prime}*(\rho^{+})^{\prime}*\rho^{0}.

Furthermore,
(\rho^{+})^{\prime}
,
(\rho^{-})^{\prime}
and
\rho^{0}
depend continuously on
\rho^{+}
and
\rho^{-}
.

Proof.

Order the coarse weights of
\Delta^{+}(a_{0})
and
\Delta^{-}(a_{0})
using a fixed circular ordering as
\Delta^{+}(a_{0})=\left\{\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n}\right\}
and
\Delta^{-}(a_{0})=\left\{\beta_{1},\dots,\beta_{m}\right\}
. Since
\Delta^{+}(a_{0})
is a stable subset,
G_{+}
is a nilpotent group by Lemma
13.3
. Therefore, we may write
\rho_{+}=u_{n}*\dots*u_{1}
for some
u_{i}\in N^{\alpha_{i}}
.
We will inductively show that we may write the product
\rho^{+}*\rho^{-}
as
u_{n}*\dots*u_{k}*(\rho^{-})^{\prime}*v_{k-1}*\dots*v_{1}*\rho^{0}
for some
v_{i}\in N^{\alpha_{i}}
,
(\rho^{-})^{\prime}\in\mathcal{G}_{-}
and
\rho^{0}\in D
(all of which depend on
k
, the index of the induction). Our given expression is the base case
k=1
.

Suppose we have this for
k
. If
-\alpha_{k}\in\Delta
, then it must be in
\Delta^{-}(a_{0})
. Let
l(k)
denote the index for which
\beta_{l(k)}=-\alpha_{k}
if
-\alpha_{k}
is a coarse weight. Otherwise, since there is no coarse weight negatively proportional to
\alpha_{k}
, we set
\beta_{l(k)}=-\alpha_{k}
with
l(k)
a half integer
so that
-\alpha_{k}
appears between
\beta_{l(k)-1/2}
and
\beta_{l(k)+1/2}
. Then decompose
\Delta
into six (possibly empty) subsets:
\left\{\alpha_{k}\right\}
,
\left\{-\alpha_{k}\right\}
,
\Delta_{1}=\left\{\alpha_{l}:l<k\right\}
,
\Delta_{2}=\left\{\alpha_{l}:l>k\right\}
,
\Delta_{3}=\left\{\beta_{l}:l<l(k)\right\}
and
\Delta_{4}=\left\{\beta_{l}:l>l(k)\right\}
. See Figure
2
.

\alpha_{k}
-\alpha_{k}
\left\{\chi:\chi(a_{0})=0\right\}
\Delta_{1}
\Delta_{2}
\Delta_{3}
\Delta_{4}

Figure 2.
Decomposing
(\mathbb{R}^{k})^{*}
into quadrants

We let
G_{\Delta_{i}}
denote the subgroup of
G
generated by the groups
N^{\beta}
,
\beta\in\Delta_{i}
. Notice that
\Delta_{-}=\Delta_{3}\cup\left\{-\alpha_{k}\right\}\cup\Delta_{4}
(with
\left\{-\alpha_{k}\right\}
omitted if there is no coarse weight of this form) is stable, so again, since
G_{-}
is nilpotent,
(\rho^{-})^{\prime}
may be expressed uniquely as
q_{3}*w*q_{4}
with
q_{3}\in G_{\Delta_{3}}
and
q_{4}\in G_{\Delta_{4}}
and
w\in N^{-\alpha_{k}}

(if
-\alpha_{k}
is not a coarse weight, we omit this term). Now,
\left\{\alpha_{k}\right\}\cup\Delta_{2}\cup\Delta_{3}
is a stable set whose associated group is nilpotent. So
u_{k}*q_{3}=q_{2}*(q_{3})^{\prime}*v_{k}^{\prime}
for some
q_{2}\in G_{\Delta_{2}}
,
(q_{3})^{\prime}\in G_{\Delta_{3}}
and
v_{k}^{\prime}\in N^{\alpha_{k}}
. Notice that by iterating some
a\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
for which
\alpha_{k}(a)=0
, and
\beta(a)<0
for all
\beta\in\Delta_{2}\cup\Delta_{3}
, we actually know that
v_{k}^{\prime}=u_{k}
. Thus, we have put our expression in the form:

\displaystyle u_{n}*\dots*u_{k}*(\rho^{-})^{\prime}*v_{k-1}*\dots*v_{1}*\rho^{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle u_{n}*\dots*u_{k}*(q_{3}*w*q_{4})*v_{k-1}*\dots*v_{1}*\rho^{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle u_{n}*\dots*(u_{k}*q_{3})*w*q_{4}*v_{k-1}*\dots*v_{1}*\rho^{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle u_{n}*\dots*u_{k+1}*(q_{2}*(q_{3})^{\prime}*v_{k}^{\prime})*w*q_{4}

\displaystyle\qquad*\,v_{k-1}*\dots*v_{1}*\rho^{0}

Now, there are two cases:
-\alpha_{k}\not\in\Delta
in which case
w
does not appear (we may take it to be
e
). If
-\alpha_{k}\in\Delta
, then as long as
v_{k}^{\prime}
and
w
are both sufficiently small,
v_{k}^{\prime}*w=w^{\prime}*v_{k}^{\prime\prime}*g
for some
w^{\prime}\in N^{-\alpha_{k}}
,
