Figure 3.
A geometric commutator

Since
W^{\left|\alpha,\beta\right|}
is a common stable manifold, it is a Hölder foliation with
C^{2}
leaves. Choose any pair of
C^{2}
discs
D_{1}\ni x,y
,
D_{2}\ni x^{\prime},q
along
W^{B}
, of dimension
\sum_{\omega\in B}\dim(E^{\omega})
transverse to
W^{A}
inside of
W^{\left|\alpha,\beta\right|}(x)
. This is possible since
x
and
y
are connected via
c_{p}^{\chi_{k}}\chi_{k}\in B^{\prime}
and
x^{\prime}
and
q
are connected via only curves tangent to coarse Lyapunov foliations corresponding to exponents in
B
. The next part of the argument crucially uses the uniform transversality of
W^{B}
and
W^{A}
within
W^{\left|\alpha,\beta\right|}(x)
. Therefore,
x^{\prime}
and
q
are the images of
x
and
y
under the
W^{A}
holonomy from
D_{1}
to
D_{2}
. In
[
56
, Section 8.3, Lemma 8.3.1]
(similar results were obtained in
[
12
, Theorem 2.2]
and
[
4
, Appendix]
) under a uniform transversality condition on
D_{1}
and
D_{2}
, there exist bi-Lipschitz coordinates for which the leaves of the foliation
\mathcal{W}^{A}
are parallel Euclidean hyperspaces. In particular, the holonomy along
W^{A}
is uniformly Lipschitz independent of the choice of
D_{1}
and
D_{2}
(due to the uniform transversality conditions),  so
d(x,y)/d(x^{\prime},q)
is bounded above and below by a constant.

We claim that
p=q
(ie, that no weight of
(B\setminus B^{\prime})\cap\Omega_{kl}
appear). Roughly, the reason is that such weights contract too slowly. Indeed, pick an element
a^{\prime}\in\ker\alpha
such that
\beta(a^{\prime})=-1
. We may perturb
a^{\prime}
to an element
a
which is regular and such that
\alpha(a)<0
, and such that if
\delta\in B\setminus B^{\prime}
,
\chi_{k}(a)<\delta(a)<0
. This is possible because if
\delta=\sigma\,\alpha+\tau\,\beta\in B\setminus B^{\prime}
, then
\tau<1
, so
\delta(a^{\prime})=-\tau>-1=\beta(a^{\prime})\geq c_{p}^{\chi_{k}}\chi_{k}(a^{\prime})
, since when
c_{p}^{\chi_{k}}\chi_{k}\in B^{\prime}
, the
\beta
coefficient is at least 1. This is clearly an open condition for each
\delta
, so we may choose
a
as indicated. We may also assume, by rescaling
a
as necessary, that
\beta(a)=-1
.

Since
y=\exp(u)x
, we can estimate distance between iterates of
x
and
y
using the intertwining property
(HA-3)
.

Recall that since
c_{p}^{\chi_{k}}\chi_{k}\in B^{\prime}
,
c_{p}^{\chi_{k}}\chi_{k}(a)<\beta(a)=-1
. Therefore
d_{W^{\chi_{k}}}(a^{t}\cdot x,a^{t}\cdot y)=e^{tc_{p}^{\chi_{k}}\chi_{k}(a)}d_{W^{\chi_{k}}}(x,y)<e^{-t}d_{W^{\chi_{k}}}(x,y)
using the partially Hölder
metric along the leaves of
W^{\chi_{k}}
. Now, suppose
p\not=q
. Recall that
p
and
q
are connected by legs in
B\setminus B^{\prime}=\left\{\delta_{1},\dots,\delta_{m}\right\}
, so that there exist
p=x_{0},x_{1},\dots,x_{m}=q
such that
x_{s}

x_{s-1}
are connected by a short curve everywhere tangent to
E^{\delta_{s}}
. Since the distributions
E^{\delta_{s}}
are transverse, if
p\not=q
, there exists some
s
for which
x_{s}\not=x_{s-1}
. Without loss of generality, we assume that
\left\{\delta_{s}\right\}
are ordered such that
0>\delta_{1}(a)>\delta_{2}(a)>\dots>\delta_{m}(a)>\chi_{i}(a)
. Then let
s_{0}
be the minimal
s
for which
x_{s}\not=x_{s-1}
, and
c_{1}=\delta_{s_{0}}(a)
,
c_{2}=\delta_{s_{0}+1}(a)
. Notice that
0>c_{1}>c_{2}>-1
. By minimality, we get
x_{s_{0}-1}=p
.

Let
d
denote the Riemannian distance on the manifold. Since for any
\chi\in\Delta
, the distance along each
W^{\chi}
leaf is locally Lipschitz equivalent to the distance on the manifold, there exists
L>0
such that for all
\chi\in\Delta
and sufficiently close points
z\in W^{\chi}(z^{\prime})
, we have
L^{-1}d_{W^{\chi}}(z,z^{\prime})\leq d(z,z^{\prime})\leq Ld_{W^{\chi}}(z,z^{\prime})
. Then after applying the triangle inequality, for sufficiently large
t
, we get:

\displaystyle d(a^{t}\cdot x^{\prime},a^{t}\cdot q)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle d(a^{t}\cdot x_{s_{0}},a^{t}\cdot p)-d(a^{t}\cdot x^{\prime},a^{t}\cdot p)-d(a^{t}\cdot x_{s_{0}},a^{t}q)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle L^{-1}d_{W^{[\delta_{s_{0}}]}}(a^{t}\cdot x_{s_{0}},a^{t}\cdot p)-d(a^{t}\cdot x^{\prime},a^{t}\cdot p)-d(a^{t}\cdot x_{s_{0}},a^{t}q)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle L^{-2}e^{c_{1}t}d(x_{s_{0}},p)-L^{2}Ce^{c_{2}t}\geq C^{\prime}e^{c_{1}t}

since by construction, apply the triangle inequality to all legs connecting
a^{t}\cdot x_{s_{0}}
and
a^{t}\cdot q
and
a^{t}\cdot x^{\prime}
and
a^{t}\cdot p
, which contract faster than
e^{c_{2}t}
and
e^{-t}
, respectively, since
c_{1}>c_{2}>-1
. We may construct new disks
D_{1,t}
and
D_{2,t}
are tangent to
W^{B}
with the same uniform transversality conditions mentioned above to
W^{A}
connecting
a^{t}\cdot x
and
a^{t}\cdot y
, and
a^{t}\cdot x^{\prime}
and
a^{t}\cdot q
(note that we may not simply iterate the disks
D_{1}
and
D_{2}
forward, since
W^{A}
is not the fast foliation for
a
, and the transversality may degenerate). Therefore, since each of the foliations along weights of
B
are uniformly transverse to those of
A
, and the holonomies are Lipschitz with a uniform Lipschitz constant on uniformly transverse discs, we arrive at a contradiction to the fact that
d(a^{t}\cdot x,a^{t}\cdot y)<C^{\prime\prime}e^{-t}
, so
p=q
.

Therefore, the connection between
w
and
y^{\prime}
only involves weights of
A\cup B^{\prime}
. By the induction hypothesis, the commutator of two weights in
(A\cup B^{\prime})\cap\Omega_{k\ell}
produces only weights in
(A\cup B^{\prime})\cap\Omega_{k\ell}
. Hence, for any
x
,

e(v)*e(u)\cdot x=\rho_{1}*e(u)*e(v)\cdot x=e(u)*e(v)*\rho_{2}\cdot x

for some paths
\rho_{1}
and
\rho_{2}
which only involve
e(w)
for some
w
in the weight spaces
A\cup B^{\prime}
, but may depend on
x
,
u
and
v
. Using such relations, for any word in the weights
A\cup B^{\prime}
, we may put it in a desired circular ordering without weights in
B\setminus B^{\prime}
. This proves the inductive step, and hence the lemma, since
B\setminus B^{\prime}=\left\{\sigma\,\alpha+\tau\,\beta:\sigma,\tau<1\right\}\cap\Omega
.
∎

14.2.
Regularity of conjugacies and proof of Theorems
2.16
and
2.18

Theorem 14.5
.

a) If
\mathbb{R}^{k}\curvearrowright X
is a totally Anosov
C^{\infty}
(resp.
C^{2}
) action
satisfying assumptions of Theorem
2.16
, then
a finite cover of the action is
C^{\infty}
(resp.
C^{1+}
) conjugate to a bi-homogeneous action on some bi-homogeneous space
K\backslash H/\Gamma
.

b) If
\mathbb{R}^{k}\curvearrowright X
is a
C^{\infty}
totally partially hyperbolic action satisfying assumptions as in Theorem
2.18
then a finite cover of the action is
C^{\infty}
conjugate to a bi-homogeneous action on some bi-homogeneous space
K\backslash H/\Gamma
.

Proof.

By Section
8
both actions in a) and b) satisfy
(GHR)
and
(HOC)
. As before we assume that leaves of coarse Lyapunov foliations are orientable (otherwise if we pass to a finite cover) so that the constructions in Section
III
apply.

By Proposition
14.1
and Theorem
11.8
, it follows that there is a
C^{0}
-conjugacy between the canonical lift
\hat{X}
produced in Theorem
10.16
and a translation action on a homogeneous space
H/\Gamma
. The
C^{0}
conjugacy between
\tilde{X}
and
H/\Gamma
induces a
C^{0}
conjugacy
h
between
X
and
K\backslash H/\Gamma
. So it suffices to show that any
C^{0}
conjugacy between
X
and
K\backslash H/\Gamma
is
C^{\infty}
(resp.
C^{1+}
).

The coarse Lyapunov manifolds are parameterized by nilpotent group actions, which are subgroups of the full group
H
in the homogeneous model (and hence act smoothly on the space
H/\Gamma
). They are conjugated to the actions
N^{\alpha}\curvearrowright X
produced in Theorem
10.16
, which act by
C^{\infty}
(resp.
C^{1+}
) diffeomorphisms on their orbits by Lemma
9.12
or
9.10
.

By standard Lie theoretic arguments (see, e.g.,
[
62
]
, Section 5.1), since each element is a diffeomorphism, the group action is
C^{1}
in both cases. In the
C^{\infty}
setting, it also follows immediately that the group action is
C^{\infty}
, and therefore provides
C^{\infty}
-coordinates. Therefore, the conjugacy is
C^{\infty}
restricted to each leaf of a coarse Lyapunov foliations.

In the
C^{2}
-case, we still have that
h
is
C^{1}
along leaves of
W^{\alpha}
. Notice that if
\left|\left|\cdot\right|\right|_{\alpha}
is the partial Hölder metric on
TW^{\alpha}
constructed using
(HOC)
in Section
9
, then it is invariant under the isometries in Proposition
9.2
. Then
h_{*}\left|\left|\cdot\right|\right|_{\alpha}
is a norm which is invariant under right translation on each leaf of
W^{\alpha}
in
H/\Gamma
(notice that while right translation is not defined on all of
H/\Gamma
, it is well-defined on each
W^{\alpha}
-leaf). In particular,
h_{*}\left|\left|\cdot\right|\right|_{\alpha}
is
C^{\infty}
on each
W^{\alpha}
-leaf of
H/\Gamma
. Therefore,
h:W^{\alpha}(x)\to W^{\alpha}(h(x))
is an isometry between a Hölder metric on the leaf in
\hat{X}
and
C^{\infty}
metric on the leaf in
H/\Gamma
, and is therefore
C^{1+}
by Theorem
5.12
.

Next we show that if
h
is
C^{r}
(
r=\infty
or
r=1+
) along all the coarse Lyapunov foliations
W^{\alpha}
, then
h
is
C^{r}
along the foliations
W^{s}_{a}
and
W^{u}_{a}
. We show it for
W^{s}_{a}
, the proof for
W^{u}_{a}
follows by considering
-a
. List the coarse exponents
\Delta^{-}(a)=\left\{\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n}\right\}
is a circular ordering, so that there are foliations
W_{i}
such that
TW_{i}=\bigoplus_{j=1}^{i}TW^{\alpha_{j}}
.
We claim that
h
is
C^{r}
along
W_{i}
by induction on
i
.

We have already established the base case of
i=1
. Assume that
h
is
C^{r}
along
W_{i}
. Then by construction,
W_{i+1}
is a foliation whose leaves have two transverse subfoliations:
W_{i}
and
W^{\alpha_{i+1}}
. Since we know that
h
is
C^{r}
along each by induction and since
W^{\alpha_{i+1}}
is a coarse Lyapunov foliation, it follows that
h
is
C^{r}
along
W_{i+1}
by Journé’s Theorem
5.11
). This proves the inductive step. Since
W^{s}_{a}=W_{n}
, it follows that
h
is
C^{r}
along
W^{s}_{a}
.

Now that we have that conjugacy has desired regularity along stable and unstable foliations, we split to two different arguments for Anosov and for partially hyperbolic case.

Proof of a).

The conjugacy
h
is clearly
C^{\infty}
(resp.
C^{1+}
) along the
\mathbb{R}^{k}
-orbits. The smoothness of
h
will now follow from iterated applications of Journé’s Theorem
5.11
. Fix an
E^{c}
-partially hyperbolic element
a
. We know
h
is
C^{\infty}
(resp.
C^{1+}
) along the foliations
W^{s}_{a}
,
W^{u}_{a}
and
\mathbb{R}^{k}
. Then by Theorem
5.11
, since
\mathbb{R}^{k}
and
W^{s}_{a}
sub-foliate the center-stable manifold as transverse subfoliations, it follows that
h
is
C^{\infty}
(resp.
C^{1+}
) along the center-stable foliation. Then again, since the conjugacy is
C^{\infty}
(resp.
C^{1+}
) along the center-stable foliation and the unstable foliation, another application Theorem
5.11
shows that
h
is
C^{\infty}
(resp.
C^{1+}
).

b) The inverse of the conjugacy

h^{-1}
is uniformly
C^{\infty}
along
algebraic
foliations
h(W^{s}_{a}),h(W^{u}_{a})
by the same argument as that we had above for
h
.
Since the tangent bundles of
h(W^{s}_{a}),h(W^{u}_{a})
with their Lie brackets generate the whole tangent bundle of
K\backslash H/\Gamma
, by Theorem 2.1
[
52
]
which using subelliptic estimates gives global regularity of functions which are regular along smooth generating distributions, we derive that
h^{-1}
is
C^{\infty}
on
K\backslash H/\Gamma
. Then it suffices to show that
Dh^{-1}
is non-degenerate everywhere.

Since
h
is a conjugacy, by taking Jacobian (with respect to) invariant volumes) of the conjugacy equation

Jac(Dh^{-1}|_{a(x)})\cdot Jac(Da|_{x})=Jac(Dh^{-1}|_{x})\cdot Jac(Da|_{h^{-1}(x)}),

where we use the same notation
a
to denote the action of
a
on the algebraic and on the non-algebraic model, keeping in mind that
x
is in the algebraic model and
h^{-1}(x)
is in the non-algebraic one.

Now by using that
\mathbb{R}^{k}\curvearrowright X
is a volume preserving action, for any
a
we have
Jac(Dh^{-1}|_{a(x)})=Jac(Dh^{-1}|_{x})
, hence
Jac(Dh^{-1})
is
a
-invariant. Because
a
is ergodic,
Jac(Dh^{-1})
is constant on a full volume set.
Since
h^{-1}
is a homeomorphism,
h^{-1}
must have non-zero Jacobian at some positive volume set. This means
Jac(Dh^{-1})
is a constant, non-zero function everywhere.

∎

Part V
Appendices

Appendix A
Normal forms for contracting foliations

We recall some aspects of normal forms theory, following
[
44
,
43
]
which contains optimal results in the
uniformly contracting setting. If
f:X\to X
is a
C^{\infty}
diffeomorphism of a Riemannian manifold with norm
\left|\left|\cdot\right|\right|
preserving a continuous foliation
\mathcal{W}
with
C^{\infty}
leaves, and
\chi=(\chi_{1},\dots,\chi_{\ell})
is an
\ell
-tuple of negative numbers such that
\chi_{1}<\dots<\chi_{\ell}<0
, we say that
f
has
(\chi,\varepsilon)
-spectrum on
\mathcal{W}
if there is a splitting
T\mathcal{W}=\mathcal{E}^{1}\oplus\dots\oplus\mathcal{E}^{\ell}
into invariant subbundles such that for every
v\in\mathcal{E}^{i}
,

e^{\chi_{i}-\varepsilon}\leq\left|\left|df(v)\right|\right|/\left|\left|v\right|\right|\leq e^{\chi_{i}+\varepsilon}.

By Remark 4.2 of
[
43
]
, this is sufficient to obtain the usual narrow band condition on the Mather spectrum if
\varepsilon
is sufficiently small. Write a vector
v\in T_{x}\mathcal{W}
in coordinates as
v=(v_{1},\dots,v_{\ell})
, where
v_{i}\in\mathcal{E}^{i}_{x}
. A polynomial
q:\mathcal{E}_{x}\to\mathcal{E}_{y}
is said to be
(s_{1},\dots,s_{\ell})
-homogeneous if

q(\lambda_{1}v_{1},\dots,\lambda_{\ell}v_{\ell})=\lambda_{1}^{s_{1}}\dots\lambda_{\ell}^{s_{\ell}}q(v_{1},\dots,v_{\ell})).

Then with a fixed
\ell
-tuple
(\chi_{1},\dots,\chi_{\ell})
, say that a polynomial
p:\mathcal{E}_{x}\to\mathcal{E}_{y}
is of
subresonance type (with respect to
\chi
)
if

p(v_{1},\dots,v_{\ell})=(p_{1}(v_{1},\dots,v_{\ell}),\dots,p_{\ell}(v_{1},\dots,v_{\ell})),

and for
i=1,\dots,\ell
,
p_{i}
is a a sum of
(s_{1},\dots,s_{\ell})
-homogeneous polynomials such that
\chi_{i}\leq\sum s_{j}\chi_{j}
. It is of
resonance type (with respect to
\chi
)
if each
p_{i}
is a sum of
(s_{1},\dots,s_{\ell})
-homogeneous polynomials such that
\sum s_{j}\chi_{j}=\chi_{i}
.
The following is a consequence of Theorem 4.6 of
[
43
]
.

Theorem A.1
.

Let
f:X\to X
be a
C^{\infty}
diffeomorphism of a
C^{\infty}
manifold
X
preserving a continuous foliation
\mathcal{W}
with
C^{\infty}
leaves. If
\chi=(\chi_{1},\dots,\chi_{\ell})
is an
\ell
-tuple as above, then there exists a constant
\varepsilon=\varepsilon(\chi)>0
with the following property: if there exists a smooth Riemannian metric for which
df|_{T\mathcal{W}}
has
(\chi,\varepsilon)
-spectrum, then there exists a family of
C^{\infty}
diffeomorphisms
\mathcal{H}_{x}:T_{x}\mathcal{W}\to\mathcal{W}(x)
such that

NF-1

for every
x\in X
,
\mathcal{H}_{f(x)}^{-1}\circ f\circ\mathcal{H}_{x}
is a subresonance polynomial,

NF-2

for every
x\in X
,
d\mathcal{H}_{x}=\mathrm{Id}
,

NF-3

if
\mathcal{G}_{x}
is any other such family, then
\mathcal{G}_{x}=\mathcal{H}_{x}\circ p_{x}
, for some family of subresonance polynomials
p_{x}
,

NF-4

if
y\in\mathcal{W}(x)
, then
\mathcal{H}_{y}=\mathcal{H}_{x}\circ q_{x,y}
for a composition of a translation with some subresonance polynomial
q_{x,y}
,

NF-5

if
g:X\to X
is a
C^{\infty}
diffeomorphism which commutes with
f
, then
\mathcal{H}_{g(x)}^{-1}\circ g\circ\mathcal{H}_{x}
is a subresonance polynomial.

Appendix B
Polynomial functions along transverse foliations

The following was shown by Margulis
(
[
59
, Lemma 4]
or
[
60
, Lemma 17]
), who proved it for rational functions in the measurable setting assuming Lebesgue almost everywhere properties (cf. also
[
84
, Theorem 3.4.4]
and its proof.) We provide a proof for polynomials in the continuous case which is much simpler and more straightforward.

Lemma B.1
.

Let
V
be a vector space, and
f:V\to\mathbb{R}
be a continuous function such that
t\mapsto f(v+tw)
is a polynomial in
t
for every
v,w\in V
. Then
f
is a polynomial.

Proof.

We prove that for any collection of linearly independent elements
v_{1},\dots,v_{n}
, the map

(t_{1},\dots,t_{n})\mapsto f(w+\sum t_{i}v_{i})

is a polynomial in
(t_{1},\dots,t_{n})
by induction on
n
. Notice that the base case of
n=1
is the assumption of the lemma, and the case of
n=\dim(V)
proves the conclusion.

The degree of the polynomials must be uniformly bounded by some constant
N
since the map
f
is continuous. Assume we have shown it for subspaces of dimension
n-1
, and consider a collection
v_{1},\dots,v_{n}
. Then by assumption, fixing
t_{2},\dots,t_{n}
and letting
t_{1}
vary yields a continuous family of polynomials in
t_{1}
, so

(B.1)

f(t_{1}v_{1}+\dots+t_{n}v_{n})=a_{0}(t_{2},\dots,t_{n})+a_{1}(t_{2},\dots,t_{n})t_{1}+\dots+a_{N}(t_{2},\dots,t_{n}){t_{1}}^{N}

for some collection of continuous functions
a_{i}:\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\to\mathbb{R}
. By induction, the functions

p_{k}(t_{2},\dots,t_{n}):=f(kv_{1}+t_{2}v_{2}+\dots+t_{n}v_{n})

are polynomials. Applying (
B.1
) to the definition of the polynomials
p_{k}
yields the following system

\begin{pmatrix}1&1&1&\dots&1\\
2&4&8&\dots&2^{N}\\
3&9&27&\dots&3^{N}\\
\vdots&&&\ddots&\\
N+1&(N+1)^{2}&(N+1)^{3}&\dots&(N+1)^{N+1}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}a_{0}\\
a_{2}\\
a_{3}\\
\vdots\\
a_{N}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}p_{1}\\
p_{2}\\
p_{3}\\
\vdots\\
p_{N+1}\end{pmatrix}

The above is a Vandermonde matrix which is invertible, so each function
a_{i}
is actually a linear combination of the polynomials
p_{1},\dots,p_{N}
. Therefore, (
B.1
) shows that the function is a polynomial.
∎

Appendix C
Generation of simple Lie groups and their Weyl groups by detected subgroups

Let
G
be a simple real Lie group,
A\subset G
be an
\mathbb{R}
-split Cartan subgroup, and
a\in A
be a non-identity element. Furthermore, let
\Delta_{G}
denote the set of restricted roots of
G
relative to the Cartan subgroup.

Definition C.1
.

We call a functional
\lambda\in A^{*}

detected by
a
if
\lambda(a)\not=0
,
positive
for
a
if
\lambda(a)>0
and
negative
for
a
if
\lambda(a)<0
. We let the sets
\Delta_{a}
,
\Delta_{a}^{+}
and
\Delta_{a}^{-}
denote the set of detected, positive and negative restricted roots for
a
respectively.

By definition,
\Delta_{a}=\Delta_{a}^{+}\cup\Delta_{a}^{-}
.

Lemma C.2
.

For any simple real Lie group
G
and non-identity element
a\in A
,
\mathfrak{u}_{a}^{\pm}:=\bigoplus_{\lambda\in\Delta_{a}^{\pm}}\mathfrak{g}_{\lambda}
are Lie subalgebras of
\operatorname{Lie}(G)
, which correspond to subgroups
U_{a}^{\pm}
.
G
is generated by
U_{a}^{+}
and
U_{a}^{-}
.

Recall that the
Weyl group
of
G
with respect to
A
is defined to be
W_{G}=N_{G}(A)/Z_{G}(A)
. The Weyl group is always finite and carries the information about the group
G
. The following comes from the standard theory of real Lie groups and restricted roots (for
\mathbb{C}
groups, see
[
39
, Lemma 10.4B]
;
\mathbb{R}
-groups are reduced to the
\mathbb{C}
-case via, e.g.,
[
80
, Proposition 1.1.3.1]
):

Proposition C.3
.

W_{G}
is generated by reflections of the form

(C.1)

w_{\beta}(\lambda)=\lambda-\dfrac{\langle\beta,\lambda\rangle}{\langle\beta,\beta\rangle}\beta

for some inner product
\langle\,,\,\rangle
on
A^{*}
. Furthermore, for every
\beta,\lambda\in\Delta_{G}
,

(C.2)

w_{\beta}w_{\lambda}w_{\beta}^{-1}=w_{w_{\beta}(\lambda)}

G
is simple if and only if
W_{G}
leaves no proper subspace of
A^{*}
invariant.

Given some non-identity element
a\in A
, let
W_{a}\subset W_{G}
denote the subgroup of
W_{G}
generated by the reflections
w_{\beta}
,
\beta\in\Delta_{a}
, and
W_{0}\subset W_{G}
denote the subgroup of
W_{G}
generated by reflections
w_{\beta}
,
\beta\not\in\Delta_{a}
(undetected roots).

Lemma C.4
.

If
w\in W_{G}
is an element, then we may write
w=w_{1}w_{2}
, where
w_{1}
is a product of reflections across undetected roots, and
w_{2}
is a product of reflections across detected roots.

Proof.

This follows from the normalization property
w_{\beta}w_{\alpha}w_{\beta}=w_{w_{\beta}(\alpha)}
, and the fact that if
\alpha
is detected and
\beta
is undetected, then
w_{\beta}(\alpha)=\alpha-c\beta
for some
c\in\mathbb{R}
is detected. Hence, we may use this commutation property to push the appearance of all undetected weights to the left.
∎

Lemma C.5
.

Let
G
be simple, and
a
be as above. For every undetected root
\beta
, there exists a detected root
\alpha
such that
w_{\alpha}(\beta)\not=\beta
.

Proof.

Assume otherwise. Then there exists an undetected root
\beta
such that for every detected root
\alpha
,
w_{\alpha}(\beta)=\beta
. Furthermore, for every undetected root
\gamma
,
w_{\gamma}(\beta)(a)=\beta(a)+c\gamma(a)=0
. It follows by Lemma
C.4
that for any
w\in W_{G}
,
w(\beta)
is undetected (the detected roots of the decomposition fix
\beta
, and the undetected ones permute among themselves). Let
V\subset A^{*}
denote the span of
\left\{w(\beta):w\in W_{G}\right\}
. Then
V
is invariant under
W_{G}
, and contained in the span of the undetected roots. This is a contradiction to the last claim of Proposition
C.3
, so the lemma holds.
∎

Corollary C.6
.

If
G
is simple,
W_{a}=W_{G}
for every non-identity element
a
.

Proof.

It suffices to show that each reflection
w_{\beta}
is generated by reflections in
W_{a}
. If
\beta
is undetected, by Lemma
C.5
, there exists a detected root
\alpha
such that
w_{\alpha}(\beta)\not=\beta
. But since
\alpha
is detected and
w_{\alpha}(\beta)=\beta-c\alpha
,
c\not=0
, it follows that
w_{\alpha}(\beta)
is detected. Therefore,

w_{\beta}=w_{w_{\alpha}(w_{\alpha}(\beta))}=w_{\alpha}w_{w_{\alpha}(\beta)}w_{\alpha}

is generated by reflections across detected roots.
∎

Corollary C.7
.

If
G
is a simple Lie group and
a\in A
is a non-identity element, then for every
\theta\in A^{*}
, there exists some
w\in W_{a}
such that
w(\theta)
is not proportional to
\theta
.

Proof.

Note that if
w(\theta)
is proportional to
\theta
for every
w\in W_{a}
, then
\mathbb{R}\theta
is an invariant subspace of
W_{a}
. The result follows immediately from the last statement of Proposition
C.3
and Corollary
C.6
and the fact that
\dim(A^{*})\geq 2
.

∎

Appendix D
Centralizers of ergodic homogeneous actions

We prove some results related to the regularity of centralizers. For homogeneous flows, the centralizers have the best regularity: affine. It is used in Section
7.4
.

Theorem D.1
.

Let
G
be a simply connected Lie group,
\phi_{t}:G/\Gamma\to G/\Gamma
be an ergodic homogeneous flow generated by an
\mathbb{R}
-semisimple element. Let
Z_{\operatorname{Lip}}
denote the set of Lipschitz transformations commuting with
\phi_{t}
. Then
Z_{\operatorname{Lip}}=Z_{\mathrm{Aff}}
, the group of affine transformations commuting with
\phi_{t}
.

The principle tool in proving Theorem
D.1
is a result of Zeghib, which we summarize here. If
X
is a
C^{\infty}
manifold, a subset
N\subset X
is called
rectifiable
if it is the Lipschitz image of a bounded subset of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
for some
n
. If
N
is a rectifiable subset, it has a well-defined Hausdorff measure, which we denote by
\mu_{N}
.

Theorem D.2

(Théorèm A,
[
83
]
)
.

Let
G
be a simply connected Lie group,
\phi_{t}:G/\Gamma\to G/\Gamma
be an ergodic homogeneous flow, generated by an
\mathbb{R}
-semisimple element. If
N\subset X
is a rectifiable,
\phi_{t}
invariant set, then
\mu_{N}
is
\phi_{t}
-invariant, and the ergodic components of
\mu_{N}
are the Haar measures on closed
H
-orbits in
G/\Gamma
, where
H
is some fixed closed subgroup
H\subset G
.

Remark D.3
.

In fact, Zeghib claims more, by working with bi-homogeneous flows, but we will only use the homogeneous flow version here.

Proof of Theorem
D.1
.

Let
f\in Z_{\operatorname{Lip}}
, and consider the graph

N=\left\{(x,f(x)):x\in G/\Gamma\right\}\subset(G\times G)/(\Gamma\times\Gamma).

By construction, since
f
commutes with
\phi_{t}
,
N
is
\phi_{t}\times\phi_{t}
-invariant. Furthermore, since
f
is Lipschitz,
N
is rectifiable. Therefore, by Theorem
D.2
,
\mu_{N}
, the Hausdorff measure on
N
, is
\phi_{t}
-invariant.
\phi_{t}
is ergodic on
G/\Gamma
,
\phi_{t}\times\phi_{t}
must be ergodic on
N
as well.

Therefore, there exists a unique subgroup
H\subset G\times G
such that
N=(g,h)\cdot H/(\Gamma\times\Gamma)
for some
(g,h)\in G\times G
. Since
(e\Gamma,f(e\Gamma))\in N
, we may without loss of generality assume
g=e
.

Finally, consider the Lipschitz transformation
\pi:N\to G/\Gamma
defined by
\pi(x,y)=x
. Since
N
is the graph of a Lipschitz transformation,
\pi
is a Lipschitz homeomorphism. This further implies that
\pi
is a diffeomorphism, since
N
is a coset of
H
. Hence,
\dim(H)=\dim(G)
, and for each
X\in\operatorname{Lie}(G)
, there exists a unique
\bar{F}(X)\in\operatorname{Lie}(G)
such that
(X,\bar{F}(X))\in\operatorname{Lie}(H)
. It follows immediately that
\bar{F}
is a Lie algebra homomorphism since
H
is a subgroup. Let
F:G\to G
denote the lift of
\bar{F}
to
G
.

It is immediate that
H=\left\{(g,F(g)):g\in G\right\}
. Therefore, from the definition of
N
, we get that
f(g\Gamma)=hF(g)\Gamma
, and
f
is affine, as claimed.
∎

Appendix E
Lifting
G
-actions

In the setting of
G
-actions, our main conclusion is to produce a bi-homogeneous action. The main technical step is to show that the action of an
\mathbb{R}
-split Cartan subgroup lifts to an action by translations on a fibration. Recall Definition
2.12
and let
B\subset A\subset G
be a genuinely higher-rank subgroup of
G
. We divide
B
into
B
-Weyl chambers by considering the connected components of
B\setminus\bigcup_{\chi\in\Delta_{G}}\ker\chi|_{B}
, where
\Delta_{G}
are the roots of
G
for the Cartan subgroup
A
.

To recover the action of
G
on the fibration, we use the following in Section
7.4
:

Theorem E.1
.

Let
r=\infty
or
2
,
G
be a real semisimple group such that every simple factor has real rank at least 2, and
A
be an
\mathbb{R}
-split Cartan subgroup of
G
.
Let
G\curvearrowright X
be a
C^{r}
action which is
B
-totally partially hyperbolic for some genuinely higher-rank subgroup
B\subset A
with common central distribution
E^{c}
.

Assume that

•

\pi:Y\to X
is a
C^{r}
fiber bundle over
X
,

•

the action of
B
lifts to a
C^{r}
action on
Y
,

•

for each
B
-Weyl chamber
\mathfrak{C}
of
B\subset G
there exists some distinguished
a\in\mathfrak{C}
such that
the lifted action of
a
on
Y
is partially hyperbolic with respect to
E_{y}=d\pi^{-1}(E^{c}(\pi(y)))
, the saturation of the common central bundle by the tangent bundle.

Then there exists some continuous action of
\tilde{G}
, the universal cover of
G
, on
Y
which is a lift of the
G
-action in the sense the if
p:\tilde{G}\to G
is the canonical projection,
\pi(g\cdot x)=p(g)\cdot\pi(x)
. Furthermore, if there exists a continuous metric on
Y
for which
db|_{T\pi^{-1}(\pi(y))}
is isometric for every
b\in B
and
y\in Y
, the
\tilde{G}
-action is
C^{r}
.

The proof of this theorem follows the scheme introduced by the first author and Katok in
[
25
]
, extended by Zhenqi Wang in
[
78
,
79
]
and made fully general by the third author in
[
75
]
. We summarize some important definitions before proceeding with the proof.

Let
\Delta_{G}
denote the set of roots
\chi
of
G
such that
\chi/2
is not a root, and for each
\chi\in\Delta_{G}
, let
U_{\chi}
denote corresponding coarse Lyapunov subgroup. We call
\chi\in\Delta_{G}

detected
if
\chi|_{B}\not\equiv 0
, and let
\Delta_{G,B}
denote the set of detected roots. As described in Section
6.1
, we consider the free product
\mathcal{P}_{B}
of the groups
U_{\chi}
, where
\chi
ranges over
\Delta_{G,B}
and
\mathcal{P}=\mathcal{P}_{A}
. Note that since the groups
U_{\chi}
generate
G
, there exists a projection
\pi_{B}:\mathcal{P}_{B}\to\tilde{G}
(with
\pi=\pi_{A}
) such that the kernel
\mathcal{C}_{B}
(with
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}_{A}
) is exactly the expressions in
\mathcal{P}_{B}
which yield contractible cycles on
G
.

Since
G
acts on
X
, any relations among the
U_{\chi}
on
G
hold on
X
as well. Importantly, the commutator relations hold: if
\chi_{1},\chi_{2}\in\Delta_{G}
are linearly independent,
u\in U_{\chi_{1}}
and
v\in U_{\chi_{2}}
, then
[u,v]\in\displaystyle{\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\chi=s\chi_{1}+t\chi_{2}\\
s,t\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}\end{subarray}}}U_{\chi}
. Hence, the commutator relations hold on
X
. The following lemma becomes of particular interest. It is based on Lemma 4.7 of
[
75
]
:

Lemma E.2
.

Let
G
be a semisimple Lie group such that every simple factor has real rank at least 2,
B\subset A
be a genuinely higher-rank subgroup of an
\mathbb{R}
-split Cartan subgroup
A
,
\mathcal{P}_{B}
denote the free product of the detected coarse Lyapunov subgroups of
G
, and
\mathcal{C}_{S,B}\subset\mathcal{P}
denote the normal closure (in
\mathcal{P}_{B}
) of the group generated by the relations
v^{-1}*u^{-1}*v*u*\rho^{-1}\in\mathcal{P}_{B}
, where
u\in U_{\chi_{1}}
,
v\in U_{\chi_{2}}
with
\chi_{1},\chi_{2}\in\Delta_{G.B}
satisfying
\chi_{1}|_{B}\not\propto\chi_{2}|_{B}
, and
\rho
is any presentation of
[u,v]
in the group
\displaystyle\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\chi=s\chi_{1}+t\chi_{2}\\
s,t\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}\end{subarray}}U_{\chi}
. Then
\mathcal{C}_{S,B}
is a co-abelian subgroup of
\mathcal{C}_{B}
, and every continuous action of
\mathcal{C}_{B}/\mathcal{C}_{S,B}
on a space of finite topological dimension is trivial.

Proof.

We first consider the case when
B=A
, so that every root is detected. By
[
26
, Theorem 1.9]
, the following short exact sequence is a perfect central extension of
\tilde{G}
, the universal cover of
G
(in fact, it is the
universal central extension
):

1\to\mathcal{C}/\mathcal{C}_{S}\to\mathcal{P}/\mathcal{C}_{S}\to\tilde{G}\to 1.

In particular, since
\mathcal{C}/\mathcal{C}_{S}
is central in
\mathcal{P}/\mathcal{C}_{S}
, it is abelian. Furthermore, assume that
\mathcal{C}/\mathcal{C}_{S}\curvearrowright X
is an action by homeomorphisms of a space of finite topological dimension. Since the group
\mathcal{C}/\mathcal{C}_{S}
is abelian, if
x\in X
,
H_{x}=\operatorname{Stab}_{\mathcal{C}/\mathcal{C}_{S}}(x)
is a normal subgroup in
\mathcal{C}/\mathcal{C}_{s}
. In fact, since it is central in
\mathcal{P}/\mathcal{C}_{S}
, it is a closed normal subgroup there as well. It follows that we have a short exact sequence of topological groups

1\to(\mathcal{C}/\mathcal{C}_{S})/H_{x}\to(\mathcal{P}/\mathcal{C}_{S})/H_{x}\to\tilde{G}\to 1

We claim that
(\mathcal{C}/\mathcal{C}_{S})/H_{x}
is a Lie group. Indeed, it is locally path connected (since
\mathcal{C}
is locally path connected; in every combinatorial cell,
\mathcal{C}
is an algebraic variety), and the evaluation map
\sigma\mapsto\sigma\cdot x
is an injective continuous map into
X
. By Theorem
6.7
,
(\mathcal{C}/\mathcal{C}_{S})/H_{x}
is a Lie group. But since
\tilde{G}
is a simply connected semisimple Lie group, it has no perfect Lie central extensions. Hence,
H_{x}=\mathcal{C}/\mathcal{C}_{S}
, and any such action is trivial.

When not every root is detected, the result follows from
[
77
, Proposition 7.9]
, which shows that when
B
is genuniely higher-rank, there is an isomorphism between
\mathcal{P}_{B}/\mathcal{P}_{S,B}
and
\mathcal{P}/\mathcal{P}_{S}
covering
\mathrm{Id}
on
\tilde{G}
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
E.1
.

Let
a\in B
be one of the distinguished elements of the Weyl chambers which lift to partially hyperbolic maps. Consider
W^{s}_{a,X}
and
W^{s}_{a,Y}
, the stable and unstable foliations of
a
as it acts on
X
and
Y
, respectively. Since the fibers of
\pi
are contained in the center foliation, it follows that
\pi|_{W^{s}_{a,Y}(y)}
is a
C^{r}
diffeomorphism. For every
\chi
, we may therefore build an action
U_{\chi}\curvearrowright Y
by choosing some
a\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
such that
\chi(a)<0
and letting
u\cdot y
denote the unique element of
W^{s}_{a,Y}(y)
which projects to
u\cdot x\in W^{s}_{a,X}(\pi(y))
. Note that the action does not depend on the choice of
a
or Weyl chamber which
a
belongs to. By the universal property of (topological) free products, we may construct an action of
\mathcal{P}_{B}
, the free product of the groups
U_{\chi}
with
\chi\in\Delta_{G,B}
, on
Y
as well (see Proposition
6.1
).

Fix any pair of linearly independent roots
\chi_{1},\chi_{2}\in\Delta_{G,B}
. Then, by linear independence, there exists
a\in B
such that
\chi_{1}(a),\chi_{2}(a)<0
. If
u\in U_{\chi_{1}}
and
v\in U_{\chi_{2}}
, then by Lemma
E.2
, there exists a
w
written as a product of elements from the groups
U_{\chi}
,
\chi=s\chi_{1}+t\chi_{2}
,
s,t\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
such that
[u,v]w^{-1}=e
, as elements in
G
. Since the relations hold on
G
, they act trivially on
X
. Furthermore, since the orbits of
U_{\chi_{1}}
,
U_{\chi_{2}}
and every group appearing in the presentation of
w
are contained in
W^{s}_{a,X}
, the relation holds when the groups act on
Y
as well.

Therefore, the restriction of the
\mathcal{P}
-action on
Y
to
\mathcal{C}_{S,B}
is trivial. Hence there exists a well-defined continuous action of
\mathcal{C}_{B}/\mathcal{C}_{S,B}
on
Y
. This action must be trivial by Lemma
E.2
. Finally, since the
\mathcal{C}_{B}
action is trivial, the action of
\mathcal{P}_{B}
induces an action of
\tilde{G}=\mathcal{P}_{B}/\mathcal{C}_{B}
on
Y
, which by construction must cover the action on
X
.

To prove the regularity of the action, notice that the lift of the actions of the groups
U_{\chi}
to
Y
is exactly determined by stable holonomies. Since, restricted to
T\pi^{-1}(\pi(y))
, the action is isometric, the stable holonomies will have arbitrarily good pinching properties. By now-standard arguments in partial hyperbolicity (see, eg,
[
64
, Theorem 6.1]
), it follows that the holonomies, and hence action of each of the groups
U_{\chi}
on
Y
is
C^{r}
. Finally, since the
U_{\chi}
generate
\tilde{G}
, every element of
\tilde{G}
is a
C^{r}
transformation of
Y
. By Theorem
5.13
, the action
\tilde{G}\curvearrowright Y
is a
C^{r}
group action.
∎

Remark E.3
.

The smoothness of the lift can be obtained from a weaker assumption, namely center bunching. To obtain a
C^{\infty}
lift, one requires a more restrictive form of center bunching than that laid out in Section
5.1
, which allows for no exponents on the fiber whatsoever (i.e., something imitating unipotent behavior). Since our application is to a compact group extension, and the action of a semisimple Lie group will always be conformal, we use this simpler version of the statement.

Appendix F
Brin-Pesin theory in low regularity

In this appendix, we develop analogs of theorems about principal bundles with compact structure groups in low regularity. Many of the statements and arguments are very similar to previous works of Brin and Pesin
[
10
]
, Wilkinson
[
81
]
and Avila, Santamaria and Viana
[
2
]
, but require special attention due to the presence of Hölder continuity only along the stable and unstable leaves.

F.1.
Principal bundles in low regularity

We start by defining precisely regularity along a foliation.

Definition F.1
.

Assume that
\mathcal{P}
is a continuous principal
K
-bundle over a
C^{2}
manifold
X
, with a compact structure group
K
, and let
\mathcal{W}
be a continuous
n
-dimensional foliation on
X
with
C^{1}
leaves. We say that
\mathcal{P}
is
\mathcal{W}
-Hölder if

(1)

there exist continuous bundle charts
\tau_{p}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{d-n}\times K\to\mathcal{P}
such that the collection
\pi\circ\tau_{p}|_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{d-n}\times\left\{e\right\}}
form a collection of continuous foliation chart of
\mathcal{F}
which are
C^{1}
along
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\left\{0\right\}\times\left\{e\right\}
, and
\tau_{p}|_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\left\{0\right\}\times K}
is uniformly Hölder continuous. We call such a chart
\tau_{p}
a
foliation-bundle chart
.

(2)

there is a family of identifications
\psi_{x,y}
between fibers
\mathcal{P}_{x}
and
\mathcal{P}_{y}
, defined for all
x
close to
y
such that

–

\psi_{x,y}
is continuous in

x,y
,

–

for all
k\in K
,
k\psi_{x,y}(p)=\psi_{x,y}(kp)
,

–

\psi_{x,y}
varies uniformly Hölder continuously as
y\in\mathcal{W}(x)
varies along the
\mathcal{W}
-leaf, and
\psi_{x,x}=\mathrm{Id}
for all
x\in X
. In particular,
d(\psi_{x,y}(p),p)<Cd(x,y)^{\theta}
for some
C>0
and
\theta>0
and all
y\in\mathcal{W}_{loc}(x)
.

Remark F.2
.

Using charts of foliation
\mathcal{W}
, (1) is just equivalent to the existence of local continuous section of
\mathcal{P}
which is Hölder along
\mathcal{W}
. We believe (2) can be induced from (1), however for simplicity we choose to state (2) separately. As Lemma
10.10
shows, (2) is not very restrictive.

F.2.
Stable holonomies

Let
X,\mathcal{P},\mathcal{W},K
as in Definition
F.1
. We further assume that

\mathbb{R}^{k}\curvearrowright\mathcal{P}
be an action by continuous bundle automorphisms of
\mathcal{P}
covering a
C^{1}

\mathbb{R}^{k}
-action on
X
which uniformly exponentially contracts
\mathcal{W}
.
Assume the
\mathbb{R}^{k}
-extension on
\mathcal{P}
is
\mathcal{W}
-Hölder
We denote both actions on
\mathcal{P}
and
X
by
a(\cdot)
for
a\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
, since from the context it will be clear which one we refer to.

If
y,x\in\mathcal{W}(x)
are sufficiently close,
p
covers
x
and
q
covers
y
, let
\kappa(p,q)
denote the unique element of
K
such that
\psi_{x,y}(p)=\kappa(p,q)q
. Note that
\kappa(k_{1}p,k_{2}q)=k_{1}\kappa(p,q)k_{2}^{-1}
.

Lemma F.3
.

If
K
is a Lie group with a bi-invariant metric
d
, then for every
\varepsilon>0
, there exists a
T\geq 0
such that if
t,s\geq T
,
y\in\mathcal{W}_{loc}(x)
,
p
covers
x
,
q
covers
y
, then
d(\kappa(a^{t}p,a^{t}q),\kappa(a^{s}p,a^{s}q))<\varepsilon
.

Proof.

For ease of notation, let
\kappa_{t}=\kappa(a^{t}p,a^{t}q)
, so
\psi_{a^{t}x,a^{t}y}(a^{t}p)=\kappa_{t}a^{t}q
. If
s>t
, since
a^{s-t}
is a principal bundle automorphism, we have

(F.1)

a^{s-t}\psi_{a^{t}x,a^{t}y}(a^{t}p)=\kappa_{t}a^{s}q.

On the other hand,
\kappa_{s}a^{s}q=\psi_{a^{s}x,a^{s}y}(a^{s}p)
and, so

(F.2)

\kappa_{t}a^{s}q=\kappa_{t}\kappa_{s}^{-1}\kappa_{s}a^{s}q=\kappa_{t}\kappa_{s}^{-1}\psi_{a^{s}x,a^{s}y}(a^{s}p),

and combining (
F.1
) and (
F.2
), we get

(F.3)

\kappa_{t}\kappa_{s}^{-1}\psi_{a^{s}x,a^{s}y}(a^{s}p)=a^{s-t}\psi_{a^{t}x,a^{t}y}(a^{t}p).

Finally, observe that
d(\psi_{a^{s}x,a^{s}y}(a^{s}p),a^{s}p)<Cd(a^{s}x,a^{s}y)^{\theta}
, and
d(\psi_{a^{t}x,a^{t}y}(a^{t}p),a^{t}p)<Cd(a^{t}x,a^{t}y)^{\theta}
. Since
p
and
q
cover points of the same stable manifold and
s>t
,
a^{s-t}
is nonexpanding on preimages of leaves of
\mathcal{W}
in the bundle, and exponentially contracting on the base manifold. It follows that
d(\psi_{a^{s}x,a^{s}y}(a^{s}p),a^{s}p)<Cd(x,y)^{\theta}e^{-\theta\lambda s}
, and

d(a^{s-t}\psi_{a^{t}x,a^{t}y}(a^{t}p),a^{s}p)\leq d(\psi_{a^{t}x,a^{t}y}(a^{t}p),a^{t}p)<Cd(x,y)^{\theta}e^{-\theta\lambda t}.

By the triangle inequality,
d(\psi_{a^{s}x,a^{s}y}(a^{s}p),a^{s-t}\psi_{a^{t}x,a^{t}y}(a^{t}p))<2Cd(x,y)^{\theta}e^{-\theta\lambda t}
, and by (
F.3
),
\kappa_{t}\kappa_{s}^{-1}
is exponentially small in
t
. The Cauchy property follows.
∎

Now we state the main result of Section
F.2
.

Proposition F.4
.

Let
X,\mathcal{P},\mathcal{W},K
be as in Definition
F.1
.
There exists a family of maps
H_{x,y}:P_{x}\to P_{y}
for
y\in\mathcal{W}(x)
such that

(1)

H_{x,x}=\mathrm{Id}
and
H_{y,z}\circ H_{x,y}=H_{x,z}
whenever
y,z\in\mathcal{W}(x)
,

(2)

H_{x,y}(kp)=kH_{x,y}(p)
,

(3)

H_{bx,by}b=bH_{x,y}
for all
b\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
, and

(4)

for all
p\in P_{x}
,
d(a^{t}p,a^{t}H_{x,y}(p))

decays in
t
.

Furthermore, the maps
H_{x,y}
are uniquely determined by these properties, and vary continuously in
x
and
y
. The topological submanifolds
\widehat{\mathcal{W}}(p):=\left\{H_{x,y}(p):y\in\mathcal{W}(x)\right\}
form an
\mathbb{R}^{k}
-invariant continuous foliation of
\mathcal{P}
which covers
\mathcal{W}
and is uniformly contracted by
a
.

Proof.

We first show uniqueness. In fact, we show that property (4) determines the maps
H_{x,y}
. Suppose that
q
and
q^{\prime}
are both points of the fiber above
y
such that
d(a^{t}p,a^{t}q)
and
d(a^{t}p,a^{t}q^{\prime})
decay. Then
q^{\prime}=kq
for some
k\in K
, and
a^{t}q^{\prime}=a^{t}kq=ka^{t}q
. Therefore,
ka^{t}q
and
a^{t}q
are close as
t\to\infty
. Since the group
K
has a bi-invariant metric, it follows that
k=e
, and
q=q^{\prime}
.

We now prove that such a family exists. We claim that it suffices to show continuous variation in
x
and
y
, the first half of condition (1), condition (2) and condition (3). That is, we can deduce condition (4) and the second half of (1) from these. To see (4), note that by (3),

a^{t}H_{x,y}(p)=H_{a^{t}x,a^{t}y}(a^{t}p)

and by continuous variation of
H_{x,y}
in
x
and
y
and the first half of (1),
d(H_{a^{t}x,a^{t}y}(a^{t}p),a^{t}p)
decays. Thus (4) is satisfied. Finally, from (4) and the triangle inequality, it follows that both
a^{t}H_{y,z}\circ H_{x,y}(p)
and
a^{t}H_{x,z}(p)
get close to
a^{t}p
. Since we showed that condition (4) determines
H_{x,y}
, it follows that
H_{y,z}\circ H_{x,y}=H_{x,z}
.

We conclude the proof by exhibiting a family of functions
H_{x,y}
which satisfy the first half of (1), condition (2) and condition (3).
We use Lemma
F.3
. Given
p
and
q
in the fibers above
x
and
y
, respectively, let
\kappa_{\infty}(p,q)=\lim_{t\to\infty}\kappa(a^{t}p,a^{t}q)
, and note that
\kappa_{\infty}(k_{1}p,k_{2}q)=k_{1}\kappa_{\infty}(p,q)k_{2}^{-1}
. Therefore, given
p
,
x
and
y
as above the point

H_{x,y}(p):=\kappa_{\infty}(p,q)q

is a well-defined map from
P_{x}
to
P_{y}
. Moreover by Lemma
F.3
we know
H_{x,y}
is a uniform limit of a family of a continuous function
\kappa_{t}
hence is continuous (in
x,y
for
y\in\mathcal{W}_{loc}(x)
) as well.

It is clear that
H_{x,x}=\mathrm{Id}
. Furthermore, (2) is easily satisfied from the fact that
\kappa_{\infty}(kp,q)=k\kappa_{\infty}(p,q)
. So we need to verify condition (3). We verify all properties when the points
x
,
y
and
z
are sufficiently close, and extend to the global leaf
\mathcal{W}
using (3) after showing them for close points. To see (3), we invoke the uniquness property established at the start of this proof, that condition (4) determines the image of
H_{bx,by}
. Then note that
q:=H_{x,y}(p)
is the point in the fiber of
y
which converges toward
p
under
a^{t}
. Then by commutativity,
bq
converges towards
bp
under
a^{t}
. Hence,
bH_{x,y}(p)=H_{bx,by}(bp)
for all
b\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
.

To show that we get the foliation
\hat{\mathcal{W}}
, we build foliation charts explicitly. Choose a foliation chart for
\mathcal{W}
on
X
, so that the horizontals of the chart are the leaves of
\mathcal{W}
,
\varphi:\mathbb{R}^{\ell}\times\mathbb{R}^{d-\ell}\to U
locally defined and centered at
(0,0)
,
U\subset\mathcal{P}
. Let
\sigma:U\to\mathcal{P}
be a local continuous section of the bundle. Then build the locally defined foliation chart

\hat{\varphi}:\mathbb{R}^{\ell}\times\mathbb{R}^{d-\ell}\times K\to\mathcal{P}\qquad\hat{\varphi}(x,y,k)=H_{\varphi(0,y),\varphi(x,y)}(k\sigma(\varphi(0,y)))

It follows from (2) and the definition of a principal bundle that since
\varphi
is locally a homeomorphism onto its image, so is
\hat{\varphi}
. Furthermore, by the construction the image of the horizontals
\left\{(x,y_{0},k_{0}):x\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell}\right\}
are exactly the leaves of
\hat{\mathcal{W}}
. Finally, using properties (1) and (2), it follows that transition maps between any two such charts respect horizontals. That is, we have a foliation which is
\mathbb{R}^{k}
-invariant by (3) and uniformly contracting under
a
by (4).
∎

F.3.
Brin-Pesin constructions

Before proving the existence of a transitive subbundle for compact group extensions, we first prove some preliminary lemmas about partitioning spaces and recurrence.

Definition F.5
.

If
X
is a compact metric space and
\mathcal{W}=\left\{\mathcal{W}(x):x\in X\right\}
is a partition of
X
(into not necessarily closed sets), we say that
\mathcal{W}
is
continuously varying
if whenever
y\in\mathcal{W}(x)
and
\varepsilon>0
, there exists some
\delta=\delta(\varepsilon,x,y)>0
such that if
d(x,x^{\prime})<\delta
, then there exists some
y^{\prime}\in\mathcal{W}(x^{\prime})
such that
d(y,y^{\prime})<\varepsilon
.

It is clear that if
\mathcal{W}
is the partition into the leaves of a continuous foliation, then
\mathcal{W}
is continuously varying. Furthermore, if one forms an accessibility equivalence relation out of a family of foliations
\mathcal{F}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{F}_{n}
, namely that
y\in\mathcal{W}(x)
if and only if there is a path with finitely many legs in the foliations
\mathcal{F}_{i}
connecting
x
and
y
, then
\mathcal{W}
is also continuously varying.

Lemma F.6
.

Let
X
be a compact metric space,
K
be a compact group,
\mathcal{G}
be a Hausdorff topological group. Assume that
K
acts on
\mathcal{G}
by automorphisms to form the semidirect product
K\ltimes\mathcal{G}
, and that there is a jointly continuous action
K\ltimes\mathcal{G}\curvearrowright X
. Furthermore, assume that
\mathcal{W}
is a continuously varying partition of
X
such that
h\mathcal{W}(x)=\mathcal{W}(hx)
for all
h\in K\ltimes\mathcal{G}
.

Assume that for some
x_{0}\in X
,
K\mathcal{W}(x_{0})
is dense and the following property holds:

(*)

for every
\varepsilon>0
, there exists a
\delta>0
and an
K
-invariant compact subset
B\subset\mathcal{G}
such that
\displaystyle\sup_{x\in X}\operatorname{diam}(B\cdot x)<\varepsilon
and that for all
x,y\in X
such that
d(x,y)<\delta
, there exists
y^{\prime}\in\mathcal{W}(x)
,
g\in B
and
k\in K
such that
gky^{\prime}=y
and
d_{K}(e,k)<\varepsilon
.

Then there is a closed subgroup
K^{\prime}\subset K
and closed sets
\mathcal{F}(\theta)\subset X
indexed by
\theta\in K/K^{\prime}
such that

(1)

each
\mathcal{F}(\theta)
is the closure of
\mathcal{W}(x)
for some
x\in X
,

(2)

for every
\theta\in K/K^{\prime}
, and
k\in K
,
k\mathcal{F}(\theta)=\mathcal{F}(k\theta)
, and

(3)

X=\bigsqcup_{\theta\in K/K^{\prime}}\mathcal{F}(\theta)
.

Proof.

Throughout we assume that the metric on
X
is
K
-invariant. This is not a loss of generality since given any metric, the metric
d_{k}(x,y)=d(kx,ky)
is equivalent (by continuity of the
K
action), and we may average to get
\tilde{d}(x,y)=\int_{K}d_{k}(x,y)\,d\mu(k)
, where
\mu
is the Haar measure on
K
. Choose a point
x_{0}\in X
such that
K\mathcal{W}(x_{0})
is dense in
X
, and set
\mathcal{F}_{0}=\overline{\mathcal{W}(x_{0})}
. Define
K^{\prime}:=\left\{k\in K:k\cdot x_{0}\in\mathcal{F}_{0}\right\}
.

We claim that
K^{\prime}\subset K
is a compact subgroup. Indeed, it is clearly closed since
\mathcal{F}_{0}
is closed, so it suffices to show that it is a semigroup since
K
is compact. To see the semigroup property, assume that
K_{1},k_{2}\in K
. Then there exist sequences
x_{n},y_{\ell}\in\mathcal{W}(x_{0})
such that
x_{n}\to k_{1}\cdot x_{0}
and
y_{\ell}\to k_{2}\cdot x_{0}
. Since the
K
-action is continuous and intertwines the sets
\mathcal{W}(x)
, it follows that
x_{n}^{\prime}:=k_{1}^{-1}x_{n}\in\mathcal{W}(k_{1}^{-1}x_{0})
converges to
x_{0}
.

Fix
\varepsilon>0
and choose
\ell_{0}
large enough so that
d(y_{\ell_{0}},m_{2}\cdot x_{0})<\varepsilon/2
. Since
\ell_{0}
is fixed and
\mathcal{W}
is continuously varying, there exists
\delta>0
such that if
d(x,x_{0})<\delta
, then there exists
y_{\ell_{0}}^{\prime}(x)\in\mathcal{W}(x)
such that
d(y_{\ell_{0}}^{\prime}(x),y_{\ell_{0}})<\varepsilon/2
. Finally, choose
n_{0}
large enough so that
d(x_{n_{0}}^{\prime},x_{0})<\delta
. Then since
x_{n_{0}}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{W}(k_{1}^{-1}x_{0})
, and
y_{\ell_{0}}^{\prime}(x_{n_{0}}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{W}(x_{n_{0}}^{\prime})
,
y_{\ell_{0}}^{\prime}:=y_{\ell_{0}}^{\prime}(x_{n_{0}}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{W}(k_{1}^{-1}(x_{0}))
, and

(F.4)

d(y_{\ell_{0}}^{\prime},k_{2}x_{0})\leq d(y_{\ell_{0}}^{\prime},y_{\ell_{0}})+d(y_{\ell_{0}},k_{2}x_{0})<\varepsilon/2+\varepsilon/2=\varepsilon.

Using
K
-invariance of the distance, and the intertwining property again, we get that
d(k_{1}y_{\ell_{0}}^{\prime},k_{1}k_{2}x_{0})<\varepsilon
, and that
k_{1}y_{\ell_{0}}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{W}(x_{0})
. Since
\varepsilon
is arbitrary we get that

k_{1}k_{2}\cdot x_{0}\in\overline{\mathcal{W}(x_{0})}=\mathcal{F}_{0},

so
K^{\prime}
is a subgroup.

We claim that the set
\mathcal{F}_{0}
is
K^{\prime}
-invariant. Indeed, assume that
y\in\mathcal{F}_{0}
and
k\in K^{\prime}
. Then there exist sequences
x_{n},y_{\ell}\in\mathcal{W}(x)
such that
x_{n}\to kx_{0}
and
y_{\ell}\to y
. As above we fix
\varepsilon>0
, and choose some
\ell_{0}
such that
d(y_{\ell_{0}},y)<\varepsilon/2
. Since
\ell_{0}
is now fixed, we may choose
\delta=\delta(x_{0},y_{\ell_{0}},\varepsilon/2)>0
such that if
d(x,x_{0})<\delta
, then there exists some
y_{\ell_{0}}^{\prime}(x)\in\mathcal{W}(x)
such that
d(y_{\ell_{0}}^{\prime}(x),y_{\ell_{0}})<\varepsilon/2
.

Finally, let
x_{n}^{\prime}:=k^{-1}x_{n}\in\mathcal{W}(k^{-1}x_{0})
. Then leveraging that
K
acts by isometries and intertwines
\mathcal{\mathcal{}}W
, we may choose
n_{0}
such that
d(x_{n_{0}}^{\prime},x_{0})<\delta
. A similar string of inequalities to (
F.4
) shows that
d(y_{\ell_{0}}^{\prime}(x_{n_{0}}^{\prime}),y)<\varepsilon
. Reapplying
k
yields that
k\cdot y\in\mathcal{F}_{0}
and
\mathcal{F}_{0}
is
K^{\prime}
-invariant.

For
\theta\in K/K^{\prime}
, we may now define
\mathcal{F}(\theta)=\theta\mathcal{F}_{0}
. Observe that this is well-defined since
\mathcal{F}_{0}
is
K^{\prime}
-invariant. The
K
-equivariance is true by definition. To see that the sets
\mathcal{F}(\theta)
cover
X
, note that for any
y\in X
, since
K\mathcal{W}(x_{0})
is dense in
X
, there exists
x_{n}\in\mathcal{\mathcal{}}W(x_{0})
and
k_{n}\in K
such that
k_{n}x_{n}\cdot x_{0}\to y
. Since
K
is compact, by passing to a subsequence, we may assume that the sequence
k_{n}
converges to some
k_{0}\in K
. But then

d(k_{0}x_{n},y)<d(k_{0}x_{n},k_{n}x_{n})+d(k_{n}x_{n},y).

The first term goes to zero since the maps induced by
k_{n}
converge to
k_{0}
in the topology of uniform convergence (since the action is jointly continuous and
X
is compact). The second goes to zero by assumption. Hence
k_{0}^{-1}y\in\mathcal{F}_{0}
, so
y\in k_{0}\mathcal{F}_{0}=\mathcal{F}(k_{0}K^{\prime})
.

So it remains only to show that the sets
\mathcal{F}(\theta)
are disjoint for different
\theta
-values. Given that
\mathcal{F}(\theta_{1})\cap\mathcal{F}(\theta_{2})\not=\emptyset
, we will show that
\theta_{1}=\theta_{2}
.

Assume that
y\in\mathcal{F}(\theta_{1})\cap\mathcal{F}(\theta_{2})
, and
\theta_{1}=k_{1}K^{\prime}
,
\theta_{2}=k_{2}K^{\prime}
. Then there exists some sequences
x_{n},x_{n}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{W}(x_{0})
such that
k_{1}x_{n},k_{2}x_{n}^{\prime}\to y
. When
n
is large enough, by (*) we may choose some
h_{n}\in\mathcal{G}
,
x_{n}^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{W}(x_{n})
and
k_{n}\in K
such that
k_{n}\to e
,
d(x_{n}^{\prime\prime},x_{n})\to 0
,
h_{n}\cdot x\to x
for all
x\in X
, and

h_{n}k_{n}k_{1}x_{n}^{\prime\prime}=k_{2}x_{n}^{\prime}.

We will find a sequence of points
y_{n}\in\mathcal{W}(x_{0})
converging to
k_{1}^{-1}k_{2}x_{0}
, implying by definition that
k_{1}K^{\prime}=k_{2}K^{\prime}
. Indeed, observe that
x_{n}^{\prime\prime}=k_{1}^{-1}h_{n}^{-1}k_{n}^{-1}k_{2}x_{n}^{\prime}
, and that since
K
normalizes both itself and
\mathcal{G}
,
x_{n}^{\prime\prime}=\hat{h}_{n}^{-1}\hat{k}_{n}^{-1}k_{1}^{-1}m_{2}x_{n}^{\prime}
for some
\hat{h}_{n}=k_{1}^{-1}h_{n}k_{1}\in\mathcal{G}
and
\hat{k}_{n}=k_{1}^{-1}k_{n}k_{1}\in K
. Let
y_{n}=\hat{h}_{n}^{-1}\hat{k}_{n}^{-1}k_{1}^{-1}k_{2}x_{0}=k_{1}^{-1}h_{n}^{-1}k_{n}^{-1}k_{2}x_{0}
, so that by the assumptions on
h_{n}
and
k_{n}
,
y_{n}\to k_{1}^{-1}k_{2}x_{0}
. Furthermore, since the
K\ltimes\mathcal{G}
-action intertwines the partition
\mathcal{W}
,
y_{n}\in\mathcal{W}(x_{n}^{\prime\prime})
. Finally, by assumption,
x_{n}^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{W}(x_{n})
and
x_{n}\in\mathcal{W}(x_{0})
, so
y_{n}\in\mathcal{W}(x_{0})
. Hence the sets
\mathcal{F}(\theta)
partition
X
. ∎

Lemma F.7
.

Let
\mathcal{P}
be a continuous principal bundle with compact structure group
K
over a base space
X
with projection map
\pi
, and
F:\mathcal{P}\to\mathcal{P}
be a continuous bundle automorphism covering a homeomorphism of the base,
f:X\to X
. Then
p\in\mathcal{P}
is
F
-recurrent if and only if
\pi(p)
is
f
-recurrent.

Proof.

First, note that if
F^{n_{k}}(p)\to p
, then by continuity of the projection,
f^{n_{k}}(x)\to x
, where
x=\pi(p)
. Hence,
F
-recurrence of
p
implies
f
-recurrence for
\pi(p)
immediately.

Now assume that
x
is recurrent, so that
f^{m_{n}}(x)\to x
on
X
, and note that
F^{m_{n}}:\pi^{-1}(x)\to\pi^{-1}(f^{m_{n}})
is a map intertwining the
K
-actions on the fibers. The family
F^{m_{n}}|_{\pi^{-1}(x)}
is hence equicontinuous and sub-converges to a translation from
\pi^{-1}(x)
to itself. Let
K^{\prime}
denote the set of translations obtained as
\lim_{n\to\infty}F^{m_{n}}|_{\pi^{-1}(x)}
for a subsequence
m_{n}
such that
m_{n}\to\infty
. We claim
K^{\prime}
is a closed sub-semigroup of
K
(and hence a closed subgroup of
K

[
7
, 6.15]
). Indeed, assume that
k_{1},k_{2}\in K
, so that
F^{m_{n}}(p)\to k_{1}\cdot p
and
F^{m_{n}^{\prime}}(p)\to k_{2}\cdot p
. We will build a subsequence
F^{\ell_{n}}
such that
F^{\ell_{n}}(p)\to k_{1}k_{2}\cdot p
.

Fix
\varepsilon>0
, and choose
m_{n_{0}}
such that
d(F^{m_{n_{0}}}(p),k_{2}\cdot p)<\varepsilon/2
. For a fixed
m_{n_{0}}
, the transformation
F^{m_{n_{0}}}
is uniformly continuous, so there exists a
\delta>0
such that if
d(p,q)<\delta
, then
d(F^{m_{n_{0}}}(q),k_{2}\cdot p)<\varepsilon
. Finally, choose
n_{1}
such that
d(F^{m_{n_{1}}^{\prime}}(p),k_{1}\cdot p)<\delta
. Then
d({k_{1}}^{-1}F^{m_{n_{1}}^{\prime}}(p),p)<\delta
and by choice of
\delta
,
d(F^{m_{n_{1}}^{\prime}}{k_{1}}^{-1}F^{m_{n_{0}}}(p)),k_{2}\cdot p)<\varepsilon
. Using the fact that
F
commutes with
K
and
K
preserves the distance
d
again, it follows that
d(F^{m_{n_{0}}+m_{n_{1}}^{\prime}}p,k_{1}k_{2}\cdot p)<\varepsilon
. It follows that there is a subsequence in the
F
-orbit of
p
which also converges to
k_{1}k_{2}\cdot p
.

Note that since
K^{\prime}
is a subgroup of
K
, it contains
e\in K
. This immediately implies that if
x
is recurrent for
f
on
X
, every point of the fiber
\pi^{-1}(x)
is recurrent for
F
on
\mathcal{P}
.
∎

Lemma F.8
.

Fix an
\mathbb{R}^{k}
action by continuous automorphisms of a continuous principal subbundle
\mathcal{P}
with compact structure group
K
covering a partially hyperbolic, totally recurrent
\mathbb{R}^{k}
-action on a manifold
X
. Further assume that for every partially hyperbolic
a\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
the foliations
\mathcal{W}^{s}_{a}
and
\mathcal{W}^{u}_{a}
on
X
lift to continuous foliations
\hat{\mathcal{W}}^{s}_{a},\hat{\mathcal{W}}^{u}_{a}
respectively on
\mathcal{P}
. Then for any partially hyperbolic element
a\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
, there is a residual set of points
p\in\mathcal{P}
such that
\overline{\mathbb{R}^{k}\cdot p}
is saturated by
\hat{\mathcal{W}}^{s}_{a},\hat{\mathcal{W}}^{u}_{a}
.

Proof.

To show this, we follow the strategy as in Lemma
[
72
, Lemma 10.2]
. Fix a partially hyperbolic element
a\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
.

By Lemma
F.7
, the set of
a
-recurrent points is a dense-
G_{\delta}
subset of
\mathcal{P}
. Hence the set of
a
-recurrent points is a dense
G_{\delta}
saturated by
K
-orbits. Finally, we build a set of points such that
\overline{\mathbb{R}^{k}\cdot p}
is saturated by stable and unstable foliations of the form
\hat{\mathcal{W}}^{s}_{a}
and
\hat{\mathcal{W}}^{u}_{a}
.

We recall a fact about topological foliations, the Kuratowski-Ulam theorem: if
R
is a residual set and
\hat{\mathcal{W}}
is a topological foliation, then there is a residual set
R^{\prime}
such that form every
p\in R^{\prime}
,
R\cap\hat{\mathcal{W}}(p)
is residual in the leaf topology of
\hat{\mathcal{W}}(p)
. Therefore, we may choose a residual set
R_{0}
such that for every
p\in R_{0}
, the set of
a
- and
(-a)
-recurrent points in
\mathcal{W}^{s}_{a}(p)
and
\mathcal{W}^{u}_{a}(p)
are both dense in their respective leaf topologies. It now follows from standard arguments (the “topological Hopf argument”) that the
\mathbb{R}^{k}
orbit closure of any point in
R_{0}
is saturated by
\hat{\mathcal{W}}^{s}_{a}(p)
and
\hat{\mathcal{W}}^{u}_{a}(p)
. See, e.g,
[
72
, Lemma 10.2]
. ∎

As a consequence of Lemma
F.8
, since there are only countably (in fact finitely) many choices of
a
with distinct
\mathcal{W}^{s}_{a}
and
\mathcal{W}^{u}_{a}
, by Baire Category theorem we have

Corollary F.9
.

Under assumptions of Lemma
F.8
, there is a residual set of points
p\in\mathcal{P}
such that
\overline{\mathbb{R}^{k}\cdot p}
is saturated by
\hat{\mathcal{W}}^{s}_{a},\hat{\mathcal{W}}^{u}_{a}
for any partially hyperbolic element
a\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
.

Proposition F.10
.

Fix an
\mathbb{R}^{k}
action by continuous automorphism of a continuous principal subbundle
\mathcal{P}
with compact structure group
K
covering a partially hyperbolic, totally recurrent
\mathbb{R}^{k}
-action on a manifold
X
such that for any
x,y\in X
, there exists
a\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
such that
ax
and
y
are connected by a path along the
\mathcal{W}^{s}_{b}

with finitely many legs (for each leg
b
could be different). Further assume for every partially hyperbolic
a\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
the foliations
\mathcal{W}^{s}_{a}
and
\mathcal{W}^{u}_{a}
lift to continuous foliations
\hat{\mathcal{W}}^{s}_{a},\hat{\mathcal{W}}^{u}_{a}
respectively on
\mathcal{P}
.
Then there exists a continuous subbundle
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{P}
with structure group
K^{\prime}\subset K
such that

•

\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
is
\mathbb{R}^{k}
-invariant.

•

For any partially hyperbolic element
a
,
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
is saturated by
\hat{\mathcal{W}}^{s}_{a},\hat{\mathcal{W}}^{u}_{a}
(as well as their finest intersections, the lift of coarse Lyapunov foliations).

•

\mathbb{R}^{k}
has a dense orbit on
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
.

•

If
\mathcal{P}^{\prime\prime}
is another bundle satisfying these conditions with associated structure group
K^{\prime\prime}
, then there exists
k\in K
such that
\mathcal{P}^{\prime\prime}=k\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
and
K^{\prime\prime}=kK^{\prime}k^{-1}
.

We fix a choice of
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
satisfying the conclusions of Proposition
F.10
, and call it the
(
\mathbb{R}^{k}
-)Brin-Pesin subbundle
(for the action). In particular for
\tilde{X}
defined Section
10.3
, by Proposition
10.8
and Proposition
F.4
,
its (
\mathbb{R}^{k}
-)Brin-Pesin subbundle is well-defined and we denote it by
\hat{X}
.

Proof.

Define an equivalence relation
\mathcal{W}
on
\mathcal{P}
by
y\in\mathcal{W}(x)
if and only if there is some
a\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
and a path with finitely many legs along the some
\hat{\mathcal{W}}^{s}_{b}
foliations (
b
of each leg could be different) connecting
ax
and
y
, so
\mathcal{W}
is actually the
\mathbb{R}^{k}
-saturated
\{\hat{\mathcal{W}}^{s}_{b_{i}}\}
-
accessibility classes. Then let
\mathcal{G}=\left\{e\right\}
and
K
act trivially on
\mathcal{G}
, so that
K\ltimes\mathcal{G}=K
acts on
\mathcal{P}
. Then all of the assumptions of Lemma
F.6
are satisfied, since
K
preserves the partition by
\mathcal{W}
, i.e.

(1)

for any
x,y\in\mathcal{P}
such that
y\in\mathbb{R}^{k}\cdot x
and any
k\in K
,
ky\in\mathbb{R}^{k}\cdot kx
since
\mathbb{R}^{k}
acting on
\mathcal{P}
by automorphisms.

(2)

for any
x,y
such that
y\in\hat{\mathcal{W}}^{s}_{b}(x)
and any
k\in K
,
ky\in\hat{\mathcal{W}}^{s}_{b}(kx)
since
y
converges towards to
x
under
b^{t}
if and only if
ky
converges towards to
kx
under
b^{t}
.

Furthermore, for any
x_{0}\in\mathcal{P}
, by assumption
K\mathcal{W}(x_{0})
is actually equal to all of
\mathcal{P}
and property (*) holds since
\mathcal{W}(x)
always covers
X
. Without loss of generality we could choose
x_{0}
that as in Corollary
F.9
.
Hence by Lemma
F.6
there exists a closed subgroup
K^{\prime}\subset K
such that the sets
\mathcal{F}(kK^{\prime})=\overline{k\mathcal{W}(x_{0})}
partition
\mathcal{P}
.

Set
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}=\mathcal{F}(K^{\prime})
. Since
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}=\overline{\mathcal{W}(x_{0})}
, it is clearly saturated by
\mathbb{R}^{k}
-orbits and saturated by
\hat{\mathcal{W}}^{s}_{b},\hat{\mathcal{W}}^{u}_{b}
, for any partially hyperbolic element
b
. By the choice of
x_{0}
, we further know that
\overline{\mathbb{R}^{k}\cdot x_{0}}=\overline{\mathcal{W}(x_{0})}
, so
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
contains a dense
\mathbb{R}^{k}
-orbit.

Now we show the uniqueness property. Let
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{P}^{\prime\prime}
be two such bundles with associated groups
K^{\prime}
and
K^{\prime\prime}
. Fix points
p^{\prime}
and
p^{\prime\prime}
such that
\overline{\mathbb{R}^{k}\cdot p^{\prime}}=\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
and
\overline{\mathbb{R}^{k}\cdot p^{\prime\prime}}=\mathcal{P}^{\prime\prime}
. Since
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{P}^{\prime\prime}
both cover
X
, there exists some
q^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{P}^{\prime\prime}
such that
q^{\prime\prime}=k_{1}p^{\prime}
for some
k_{1}\in K
. Then
\overline{\mathbb{R}^{k}\cdot q^{\prime\prime}}\subset\mathcal{P}^{\prime\prime}
and hence
k_{1}\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{P}^{\prime\prime}
. It follows that
k_{1}K^{\prime}k_{1}^{-1}\subset K^{\prime\prime}
. By a symmetric argument, there exists some
k_{2}\in K
such that
k_{2}\mathcal{P}^{\prime\prime}\subset\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
, and hence
k_{2}K^{\prime\prime}k_{2}^{-1}\subset K^{\prime}
. It follows that
\operatorname{Ad}(k_{1})
must take
\operatorname{Lie}(K^{\prime})
into
\operatorname{Lie}(K^{\prime\prime})
injectively, and
\operatorname{Ad}(k_{2})
takes
\operatorname{Lie}(K^{\prime\prime})
into
\operatorname{Lie}(K^{\prime})
injectively. Hence they have the same dimension and are isomorphic. Furthermore, the conjugations induce a map on the connected components of
K^{\prime}
and
K^{\prime\prime}
, which are finite sets, so we conclude that conjugation by
k_{1}
is an isomorphism between
K^{\prime}
and
K^{\prime\prime}
. The result follows.∎
